Degeneracy is OK: Logarithmic Regret for Network Revenue Management with Indiscrete Distributions
Jiashuo Jiang † \text{Jiashuo Jiang}^{\dagger} Will Ma ‡ \text{Will Ma}^{\ddagger} Jiawei Zhang § \text{Jiawei Zhang}^{\lx@sectionsign}

Abstract
We study the classical Network Revenue Management (NRM) problem with accept/reject decisions and T T IID arrivals. We consider a distributional form where each arrival must fall under a finite number of possible categories, each with a deterministic resource consumption vector, but a random value distributed continuously over an interval. We develop an online algorithm that achieves O ⁡ ( log 2 ⁡ T ) O(\log^{2}T) regret under this model, with the only (necessary) assumption being that the probability densities are bounded away from 0. We derive a second result that achieves O ⁡ ( log ⁡ T ) O(\log T) regret under an additional assumption of second-order growth. To our knowledge, these are the first results achieving logarithmic-level regret in an NRM model with continuous values that do not require any kind of “non-degeneracy” assumptions. Our results are achieved via new techniques including a new method of bounding myopic regret, a “semi-fluid” relaxation of the offline allocation, and an improved bound on the “dual convergence”.

1
Introduction

Network Revenue Management (NRM) is a capacity control problem in which limited resources are to be allocated over a finite time horizon of length
T
.
During each time step
t=1,\ldots,T
, a query
t
arrives, demanding a vector
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
of resources and providing a reward
\tilde{r}_{t}
.
An irrevocable decision must then be made about whether to serve query
t
, in which case
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
would be subtracted from the resources and
\tilde{r}_{t}
would be collected.
Query
t
is only feasible to serve if the remaining resources exceed
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
component-wise, and a feasible query
t
can still be judiciously rejected, e.g. if
\tilde{r}_{t}
is low compared to the resources consumed in
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
.
The goal is to maximize the total reward collected from serving queries using the initial resource capacities, when the values
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
for each query
t
are unknown before it arrives but known to be drawn IID across time.

Due to the curse of dimensionality in this problem, a mathematically rich literature has evolved out of developing heuristics and obtaining guarantees on their performance. We consider the stream of literature that analyzes
regret
, which is the additive loss of an online allocation algorithm compared to an optimal offline allocation that knows all values of
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
beforehand, taken in expectation over the IID query draws (and any further randomness in the algorithm).
Generally, regret is larger for longer time horizons
T
, and this literature is concerned with how the regret grows as a function of
T
when all other system parameters stay fixed (but the initial resource capacities are also allowed to scale arbitrarily with
T
).

Algorithms with
O(\sqrt{T})
regret have been known for several decades, as we discuss in
Section

1.2
.
Since then, many papers have developed algorithms with
\tilde{O}(1)
regret, which guarantees the regret to grow logarithmically or slower in
T
, under either of the following two
kinds of assumptions.
The first involves having a
small number of possible realizations
for the vector
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
, described by a discrete distribution on
N
points.
As
T
grows,
N
stays fixed and is treated as a constant in the analysis.
However, such an assumption abandons some natural models, e.g. that of
\tilde{r}_{t}
being drawn uniformly from [0,1].
On the other hand, papers that can capture these continuous distributions require a different set of assumptions, which we will call
non-degeneracy
.
At a high level, these papers assume that the mathematical program being re-solved by the online algorithm over time to make its decisions is always well-behaved, and has a unique optimal solution.
However, such assumptions appear to be motivated primarily by the analysis, and are difficult to intuit or verify, or even state. We defer their extended statements to
Appendix

B
, where we also explain why perturbation attempts to overcome degeneracy would incur a regret of
\Omega(\sqrt{T})
.

Our contribution.
We establish logarithmic regret in a natural model of Network Revenue Management that makes neither the small-
N
nor non-degeneracy assumptions.

To our knowledge, such a result has not previously existed in the literature, which we now review.
Our result builds upon the existing literature that establishes
logarithmic-or-better regret in either the small-
N
or non-degenerate settings, or in the multi-secretary special case.

NRM with small-
N
.

Jasin and Kumar 2012
initially establish a constant
O(1)
regret under
both
the small-
N
and non-degeneracy assumptions.

Bumpensanti and Wang 2020
and
Vera and Banerjee 2021
were the first to establish
O(1)
regret for a general NRM problem without any non-degeneracy assumptions. We note that
Arlotto and Gurvich 2019
first established
O(1)
regret in the
multi-secretary

special case, where all queries demand one unit of a single resource (i.e.
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}=(1)
w.p. 1).
These are all surprising results, in that given a fixed discrete distribution for
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
, the regret is upper-bounded by an absolute numerical constant, regardless of how long a time horizon
T
(and correspondingly large resource capacities) over which regret can be incurred.

Multi-secretary with general distributions.

A caveat to the aforementioned analysis is that the
O(1)
constant depends on
N
, and
N
can be
\infty
,
e.g. for continuous distributions.
To understand continuous distributions better,
Bray 2019
studies the example of
\tilde{r}_{t}
being drawn uniformly from [0,1] in the multi-secretary special case. Both
Lueker 1998
and
Bray 2019
establish an upper bound on regret that grows logarithmically with
T
, and importantly, show this regret rate of
\Theta(\log T)
to be
tight
—that is, a constant regret is no longer possible.
Recently,
Besbes et al. 2022
make further progress on the multi-secretary problem by establishing a notion of complexity for general distributions, which affects regret.

NRM with general distributions and non-degeneracy.

Although the preceding papers consider continuous and general reward distributions, it is unclear how they extend beyond the multi-secretary special case.
Meanwhile, several papers
(
Li and Ye 2021
,
Balseiro et al. 2021
,
Bray 2022
)
have studied general distributions for NRM, under a non-degeneracy condition that guarantees the binding constraints of the fluid relaxation to remain unchanged when the right-hand-side constraint is being replaced by the real-time per period remaining capacities (see
Appendix

B
). However, this requires that the real-time per period remaining capacities stay in a neighborhood of the initial per period remaining capacities. In order to guarantee this, the previous literature needs to assume that the initial capacities scale linearly with
T
. This assumption would require: i) the resource with a non-binding constraint in the fluid relaxation to have a buffer capacity that equals
\delta T
for some fixed constant
\delta>0
, and; ii) the resource with a binding constraint in the fluid relaxation to have a unique optimal dual variable lower bounded by
\delta
, where the optimal dual variable remains fixed regardless of how large
T
is since initial capacities scale linearly in
T
(the regret would scale polynomially in
1/\delta
). However, the square-root law of inventory in practice suggests that the buffer capacity should generally scale as
O(\sqrt{T})
, violating i); and if all resource constraints are binding, then the optimal dual variables can be non-unique or arbitrarily close to
0
.

All in all, as highlighted in
Bumpensanti and Wang 2020
, degeneracy is likely to occur in practice, and it has remained unknown whether a logarithmic level regret can be achieved in NRM with indiscrete distributions and without non-degeneracy.

1.1
Logarithmic Regret for NRM with Discrete Demands and Continuous Rewards

We focus on the following structural form for the distribution of
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
.
First, the demand vector
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
is drawn from a discrete distribution supported on finitely many possibilities
\bm{a}_{1},\ldots,\bm{a}_{n}
.
Then, conditional on
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}=\bm{a}_{j}
for any
j=1,\ldots,n
, the reward
\tilde{r}_{t}
is drawn from a continuous distribution
F_{j}
supposed on an interval
[l_{j},u_{j}]
whose density is lower bounded by a constant
\alpha>0
.

Our main result (
Section

3
) is to develop an algorithm with
O(\log^{2}T)
regret, assuming only this structural form for the distribution of
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
.
We note that finiteness of
n
and the density lower bound
\alpha
are prevalent conditions to get a regret that is sub-polynomial in
T

(
Besbes et al. 2022
, see)
.
And although
n
is finite, the continuous densities still induce an infinite support for the realization of vector
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
—therefore, we have successfully established logarithmic level regret in a model of NRM that allows for infinitely many possible realizations, without assuming non-degeneracy.

Our model has a natural practical interpretation—there is a discrete list of
n
flight itineraries, and a continuous range of prices
[l_{j},u_{j}]
that customers are willing to pay for each itinerary
j
.
And although we have been describing accept/reject formulations of NRM,
we can also analyze pricing formulations—where a price must be posted before seeing the customer willingness-to-pay
\tilde{r}_{t}
, and that price is collected as reward if and only if it is no greater than
\tilde{r}_{t}
. Our results will translate to the pricing formulation as long as demands are
“independent”
—that is, each customer is interested in a specific itinerary
j
and whether they purchase it does not depend on the prices of itineraries
j^{\prime}\neq j
.
This translation is achieved through the standard reduction of virtual valuations, as we detail in
Appendix

C
.
We should note that pricing was the original formulation of NRM
(
Gallego and Van Ryzin 1997
)
, in which logarithmic
(
Jasin 2014
)
and constant
(
Wang and Wang 2022
)
regret is known even without the independent demand assumption; however, these papers do need the non-degeneracy assumption.

The
O(\log^{2}T)
regret established in our main result can also be seen as an extension of a corollary of
Besbes et al. 2022
.
To elaborate, in our model two different indices
j,j^{\prime}
can have
\bm{a}_{j}=\bm{a}_{j^{\prime}}
; moreover, they can have
non-overlapping
reward intervals with
l_{j}<u_{j}<l_{j^{\prime}}<u_{j^{\prime}}
. Thus, with a single resource and
\bm{a}_{j}=(1)
for all
j=1,\ldots,n
, we can capture the multi-secretary reward distribution with density lower-bounded over disjoint intervals, for which
Besbes et al. 2022
already established a
O(\log^{2}T)
regret.

Our second result (
Section

4
) is to derive an
O(\log T)
regret bound with additional assumptions that require the density to be upper bounded and guarantee the strong convexity of the Lagrangian dual function of the fluid approximation. We also show that the constant term in this
O(\log T)
regret bound depends polynomially on all of the problem parameters, whereas in contrast, the constant term in our
O(\log^{2}T)
regret bound depended exponentially on
n
.

Aside from achieving improved regret bounds, we outline how our algorithms differ from the literature.
For our algorithm in
Section

3
, the relaxation that it is re-solving over time is different—it is re-solving a new relaxation that we call the
“semi-fluid”
relaxation of the offline optimum. We then round the solution to this relaxation, obtaining a brand new algorithm.
By contrast, our algorithm in
Section

4
is the same as the classical
“certainty-equivalent policy”
(e.g.
Balseiro et al. 2021
,
Li and Ye 2021
,
Bray 2022
) that re-solves the fluid upper bound and uses its optimal (dual) solution to guide our decision. Our innovation is in the theoretical analysis, where we show that the certainty-equivalent policy can obtain the logarithmic regret with additional conditions, but without requiring strict complementary slackness of the fluid approximation.

1.2
Further Related Work

The network revenue management (NRM) problem has been extensively studied in the literature and one main topic is to develop near-optimal policies with strong theoretical guarantees. One common way is to derive the policy from the optimal solution of the ex-ante relaxation. To be specific,
Talluri and Van Ryzin 1998
propose a static bid-price policy based on the optimal dual variable of the ex-ante relaxation and proves that the regret bound is
O(\sqrt{T})
. Then, a dynamic update of the bid-price is considered in the literature. Subsequently,

Reiman and Wang 2008
shows that by re-solving the ex-ante relaxation once to update the bid-price, one can obtain an improved regret bound
o(\sqrt{T})
. Then,
Jasin and Kumar 2012
shows that under a non-degeneracy condition for the ex-ante relaxation, a policy which re-solves the ex-ante relaxation at each time period will lead to an
O(1)
regret. The relationship between the performances of the control policies and the number of times of re-solving the ex-ante relaxation is further discussed in their later paper
(
Jasin and Kumar 2013
)
. More recently,
Bumpensanti and Wang 2020
proposes an infrequent re-solving policy and shows a regret bound of
O(1)
without the “non-degeneracy” assumption. This has been extended by
Balseiro and Xia 2022
to fair allocation problems. With a different approach,
Vera and Banerjee 2021
proves the same
O(1)
upper bound for the NRM problem and their approach is further generalized in series of papers (e.g.
Freund and Banerjee 2019
,
Vera et al. 2021
,
Freund and Zhao 2022
). Recent studies on the NRM problem includes variants such as the reusable resource setting
(
Baek and Ma 2022
)
, unknown distribution setting
(
Li et al. 2020
,
Balseiro et al. 2022
)
and imperfect distribution knowledge setting under a non-stationary environment
(
Jiang et al. 2020
)
.

Another problem that is closely related to the NRM problem is called the online packing problem, where a more general formulation is studied and less distribution knowledge is assumed. The packing problem covers a wide range of applications, including secretary problem
(
Ferguson 1989
,
Arlotto and Gurvich 2019
)
, online knapsack problem
(
Arlotto and Xie 2020
,
Jiang and Zhang 2020
)
, resource allocation problem
(
Asadpour et al. 2020
)
, network routing problem
(
Buchbinder and Naor 2009
)
, matching problem
(
Mehta et al. 2007
)
, etc. The problem is usually studied under either a stochastic model where the reward and size of each query are drawn independently from an unknown distribution
\mathcal{P}
, or a more general random permutation model where the queries arrive in a random order
(
Molinaro and Ravi 2014
,
Agrawal et al. 2014
,
Kesselheim et al. 2014
,
Gupta and Molinaro 2014
)
.

Our work comes subsequent to
Besbes et al. 2022
, but has the following relation with an extended version of their work
(
Besbes et al. 2023
)
. In the extended version, they develop a general and practical framework, Repeatedly Act using Multiple Simulations (RAMS), that simulates future demand scenarios to guide online decisions. Their framework presents a new way to derive our algorithms and bounds.

2
Problem Formulation and Our Approach

We consider an online resource allocation problem, where there are
m
resources and each resource
i\in[m]
has an initial fractional capacity
C_{i}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. There are
T
discrete time periods and at each period
t\in[T]
, one query arrives, denoted by query
t
. Each query
t
has a
random
size
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}=(\tilde{a}_{t,1},\dots,\tilde{a}_{t,m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
, where
\tilde{a}_{t,i}
denotes how much resource
i
will be consumed if query
t
is served, for all
i\in[m]
, and a
random
reward
\tilde{r}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
that denotes how much reward can be collected by serving query
t
. We assume that the value of
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
for each
t\in[T]
is drawn
independently
from an identical distribution denoted by
F(\cdot)
. We suppose that the queries are of finite types
1
1

1

Here we are using a different meaning of
“type”
than
Besbes et al. 2022
. According to their meaning, because we have a continuum of possible realizations for
\tilde{r}_{t}
, we would have infinitely many types.
, i.e., for each
t\in[T]
,
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
is supported on a finite set
\mathcal{A}=\{\bm{a}_{1},\dots,\bm{a}_{n}\}
. We call the situation where
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
is realized as
\bm{a}_{j}
as query
t
being of type
j
and we denote by
p_{j}=P(\tilde{\bm{a}}=\bm{a}_{j})
, for each
j\in[n]
.

After query
t
arrives and the value of
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
is revealed, the decision maker has to decide immediately and irrevocably whether or not to serve query
t
. Note that query
t
can only be served if for every resource
i
its remaining capacity it at least
\tilde{a}_{t,i}
. The goal of the decision maker is to maximize the total collected reward subject to the resource capacity constraint.

Any online policy
\pi
for the decision maker is specified by a set of decision variables
\{\tilde{x}_{t}^{\pi}\}_{\forall t\in[T]}
, where
\tilde{x}_{t}^{\pi}
is a binary variable and denotes whether query
t
is served, for all
t\in[T]
. Note that
\tilde{x}_{t}^{\pi}
can be stochastic if
\pi
is a randomized policy. Any policy
\pi
is feasible if for all
t\in[T]
,
\tilde{x}_{t}^{\pi}
depends only on
F(\cdot)
and
\{(\tilde{r}_{1},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{1}),\dots,(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})\}
, and the following capacity constraint is satisfied:

\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{a}_{t,i}\cdot\tilde{x}_{t}^{\pi}\leq C_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m].

(1)

The total collected value of policy
\pi
is given by
V^{\pi}(I)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{r}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}_{t}^{\pi}
, where
I=\{(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})\}_{t=1}^{T}
denotes the problem instance.

The benchmark is the prophet, which is an offline decision maker that is aware of the value of
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
for all
t\in[T]
and always makes the optimal decision in hindsight. We denote by
\{\tilde{x}^{\text{off}}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
the offline decision of the prophet, which is an optimal solution to the following offline problem:

\displaystyle V^{\text{off}}(I)=

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{r}_{t}\cdot x_{t}

(2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.t.\ }

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\tilde{a}_{t,i}\cdot x_{t}\leq C_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m]

\displaystyle x_{t}\in\{0,1\}\quad\quad\forall t\in[T].

For any feasible online policy
\pi
, we use
regret
to measure its performance, which is defined as follows:

\text{Regret}(\pi):=\mathbb{E}_{I\sim F}[V^{\text{off}}(I)]-\mathbb{E}_{I\sim F}[V^{\pi}(I)]

(3)

where
I=\{(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{a}_{t})\}_{t=1}^{T}\sim F
denotes that
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{a}_{a})
follows distribution
F(\cdot)
independently for each
t\in[T]
.
In what follows, we describe our general approach to upper bound the regret defined in (
3
), and discuss how our approach implies online policies under various settings.

2.1
O(\log^{2}T)
Regret with Lower-bounded Densities

Our
O(\log^{2}T)
result tries to extend the approach of
Vera and Banerjee 2021
from discrete distributions, which we now recap.
Fix some remaining resource capacities and suppose there are
s
time steps left.
The offline allocation knows for every possible realization
(r,\bm{a})
, called a “type”, the remaining number of queries
t
with
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})=(r,\bm{a})
, denoted by
\tilde{d}_{(r,\bm{a})}
.
Meanwhile, consider an online algorithm that solves for an optimal
fluid
packing, which replaces each
\tilde{d}_{(r,\bm{a})}
with its expectation to specify the queries that can be served.
The authors compare
\tilde{y}_{(r,\bm{a})}
, the number of queries of type
(r,\bm{a})
accepted by the offline, to
\hat{y}_{(r,\bm{a})}
, the number of such queries instructed by the fluid packing to accept.
Their key argument is that since the number of queries of type
(r,\bm{a})
is growing linearly in
s
and any two of them are interchangeable, the online algorithm only “makes a mistake” if
\tilde{y}_{(r,\bm{a})}
and
\hat{y}_{(r,\bm{a})}
are distance
\Omega(s)
apart. This is a highly unlikely event (over the randomness in the offline’s draws of
\tilde{d}_{(r,\bm{a})}
) because
\tilde{y}_{(r,\bm{a})}
and
\hat{y}_{(r,\bm{a})}
are generally only
O(\sqrt{s})
apart, specifically an event with probability
O(e^{-s})
that when summed over
s
leads to constant regret.

A new, “semi-fluid” relaxation of offline.

Comparing
\tilde{y}_{(r,\bm{a})}
to
\hat{y}_{(r,\bm{a})}
is meaningless under continuous rewards, because there is zero probability of drawing any specific
(r,\bm{a})
.
To cope, we introduce a new
semi-fluid
relaxation that amalgamates decisions over queries with the same demand vector.
Specifically, we call each
j=1,\ldots,n
a
type
in our model of NRM, and let
\tilde{d}_{j}
denote the remaining number of queries with
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}=\bm{a}_{j}
and
\tilde{r}_{t}
drawn from
F_{j}
.
The semi-fluid relaxation knows
\tilde{d}_{j}
for all
j
.
However, the semi-fluid relaxation differs from the offline allocation in that it collects exactly the “fluid” value

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{j}\int_{1-\tilde{y}_{j}/\tilde{d}_{j}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq

(4)

when it accepts
\tilde{y}_{j}
queries of type
j
. Objective (
4
) integrates over the
\tilde{y}_{j}/\tilde{d}_{j}
proportion of the
\tilde{d}_{j}
type-
j
queries with the highest rewards, as explained in
Section

3
.
Meanwhile, our algorithm is still based on solving the (fully) fluid packing, whose variables can also be amalgamated into acceptance quantities
\hat{y}_{j}
for each type
j
(and
\tilde{d}_{j}
will be replaced
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}]
, including in the objective (
4
)).
We can then compare the acceptance quantities
\tilde{y}_{j}
to
\hat{y}_{j}
for the amalgamated types
j
.

Bounding the myopic regret.

Unfortunately, because in our model queries of the same type have different rewards, a mistake can be made without requiring
|\tilde{y}_{j}-\hat{y}_{j}|=\Omega(s)
.
In fact, a mistake only requires drawing a quantile that is above the acceptance proportion
\tilde{y}_{j}/\tilde{d}_{j}
for the semi-fluid but below
\hat{y}_{j}/\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}]
for the fluid (or vice versa), which occurs with probability
|\tilde{y}_{j}/\tilde{d}_{j}-\hat{y}_{j}/\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}]|
.
As such, the rough argument that
\tilde{y}_{j}
and
\hat{y}_{j}
are
O(\sqrt{s})
apart (and
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}]=\Omega(s)
) would lead to a mistake probability of
O(1/\sqrt{s})
, and an undesirable overall regret of
O(\sqrt{T})
.
Therefore, we instead follow
Bray 2019
who argues that for continuous distributions one must quantify the “myopic regret” at each time step (instead of just bounding the probability that it is non-zero).
We decompose overall regret in way (see
Section

2
) such that the regret at a time step can be quanfied as

\displaystyle\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}+\mathbf{e}_{j})-\int_{1-\hat{y}_{j}/\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}]}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq-\frac{\hat{y}_{j}}{\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}]}\cdot\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j}}(\tilde{\bm{d}})-\left(1-\frac{\hat{y}_{j}}{\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}]}\right)\cdot\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}),

(5)

where
\bm{c}
denotes the remaining resource capacities,

j
denotes the type of the current query, and

\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
denotes the optimal objective value of the semi-fluid relaxation given remaining resources
\bm{c}
and a generic vector
\bm{d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})
counting the remaining queries of each type.

To upper-bound (
5
), we take an optimal solution
\tilde{\bm{y}}=(\tilde{y}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{y}_{n})
for
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}+\mathbf{e}_{j})
and modify it into feasible solutions for
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j}}(\tilde{\bm{d}})
and
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}})
, which lower-bounds the latter quantities.
As long as these feasible solutions can be constructed by modifying only the
j
’th coordinate of
\tilde{\bm{y}}
, we show that (
5
) is
O((\tilde{y}_{j}/\tilde{d}_{j}-\hat{y}_{j}/\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}])^{2})
. Per the earlier discussion, this is roughly
O((1/\sqrt{s})^{2})=O(1/s)
, which when summed over
s
would sufficiently lead to logarithmic regret.

A boundary-attracted algorithm.

If the semi-fluid solution
\tilde{y}_{j}
is close to 0, however, then
\tilde{\bm{y}}
is difficult to modify into a feasible solution for
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j}}(\tilde{\bm{d}})
—one cannot pack into the reduced capacity
\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j}
by only reducing coordinate
\tilde{y}_{j}
.
Our strategy is to bypass this boundary situation by tweaking the online algorithm—if there is a risk of this infeasibility, which we show can be identified by the algorithm checking whether
\hat{y}_{j}=O(\sqrt{s\log s})
, then it
always rejects
the current type-
j
query.
This effectively sets
\hat{y}_{j}=0
in (
5
) and avoids having to lower-bound the quantity
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j}}(\tilde{\bm{d}})
.
On the other extreme, our algorithm
always accepts
if
\hat{y}_{j}
is within
O(\sqrt{s\log s})
of its maximum value
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}]
.
All in all, we use this tweaked version of the fluid re-solving algorithm that is “attracted to boundaries”, which is similar in spirit to the thresholding in
Bumpensanti and Wang 2020
and the “conservatism with respect to gaps” in
Besbes et al. 2022
. We provide a new explanation for it based on our analysis, and it allows us to always upper-bound (
5
) by
O((\tilde{y}_{j}/\tilde{d}_{j}-\hat{y}_{j}/\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}])^{2})=O(1/s)
while sacrificing only a log-factor—ultimately achieving
O(\log^{2}T)
regret.

Lipschitz property for semi-fluid convex program.

Finally, it should not be taken for granted that
\tilde{y}_{j}
and
\hat{y}_{j}
are nearby, an intuition we have been frequently using.
Indeed, they correspond to optimal solutions of mathematical programs with different objective functions—the fluid problem replaces
\tilde{d}_{j}
with
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{d}_{j}]
in (
4
)—and a simple example
(
Mangasarian and Shiau 1987
, Remark 2.7)
reveals that optimal solution sets are highly sensitive to small perturbations in the objective.
Nonetheless, we extend (
Lemma

3.4
) the Lipschitz analysis of
Mangasarian and Shiau 1987
to show that the specific objective function (
4
) is well-behaved.
We also note that due to degeneracy, it is necessary for the Lipschitz property to be of the form “given any optimal solution
\hat{\bm{y}}=(\hat{y}_{1},\ldots,\hat{y}_{n})
to the fluid,
there exists
a nearby optimal solution
\tilde{\bm{y}}
to the semi-fluid”.
General perturbation analysis results for convex programs
(
Bonnans and Shapiro 2013
)
, which try to argue that
all
optimal solutions
\tilde{\bm{y}}
are nearby, do not apply in our setting with degeneracy.

2.2
O(\log T)
Regret with Bounded Densities and Second-order Growth Assumption

Based on the structural form introduced previously, we further assume that the reward density is also upper bounded, conditional on
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}=\bm{a}_{j}
, and additionally, we impose a second-order growth condition over the Lagrangian dual function of the ex-ante relaxation, which requires the dual function to be strongly convex. As discussed earlier, the second-order growth condition, together with the non-degeneracy assumption, lead to the
O(\log T\log\log T)
regret bound in
Li and Ye 2021
and the
O(\log T)
regret bound in
Balseiro et al. 2021
and
Bray 2022
. In comparison to the aforementioned papers, our contribution here is to derive an
O(\log T)
regret bound by relaxing the non-degeneracy assumption. We now explain at a high level our approach.

A tighter relaxation of the offline allocation.

Our improvement comes from using a tighter relaxation than the ex-ante relaxation as an upper bound of the offline allocation. Our relaxation is that for each sample path, we relax the integral decision of the offline allocation to be fractional, and we take an expectation over the sample path. Such an LP relaxation of the offline allocation has been derived in
Bumpensanti and Wang 2020
and
Vera and Banerjee 2021
in the discrete setting and we derive it here for general distributions. Then, by following a myopic regret approach described in
Section

3.1
, we are able to bound the regret incurred at each period by the variance of the dual variable of the LP relaxation of the offline allocation, no matter what the remaining capacities are. Note that we do not need to consider the optimal basis of the ex-ante relaxation to bound the myopic regret. This is the key distinction between our approach and the martingale-based approach in
Li and Ye 2021
,
Balseiro et al. 2021
,
Bray 2022
, which would require a non-degeneracy assumption to guarantee the optimal basis remains fixed in their analysis.

A dual convergence bound.

A second element of our approach is the dual convergence bound, which regards the variance of the dual variable of the LP relaxation of the offline allocation. To be specific, when there are
s
data points, we prove the dual convergence bound to be at the order of
O(\frac{1}{s})
. To obtain this result, we utilize both ways of splitting the whole space into a set of small cubes with exponentially increasing edge lengths in
Huber 1967
and
Li and Ye 2021
. Denote by
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}
the dual variable of the sample average problem and
\hat{\bm{\mu}}
the dual variable of the ex-ante relaxation. Then, we apply the approach in
Huber 1967
to obtain a bound on
P(|\tilde{\bm{\mu}}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}|\geq\varepsilon)
. However, we note that when
\varepsilon>\sqrt{\frac{1}{s}}
, the approach in
Li and Ye 2021
would give us a tighter probability bound. Therefore, by applying different ways to bound
P(|\tilde{\bm{\mu}}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}|\geq\varepsilon)
for different ranges of
\varepsilon
, we get a
O(\frac{1}{s})
bound on the dual convergence
\mathbb{E}[\|\tilde{\bm{\mu}}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}]
.

We do note that the “dual convergence” bound is used in different ways between our analysis vs.
Li and Ye 2021
. The data points for “dual convergence” in
Li and Ye 2021
comes from past periods. By contrast, the “data points” for “dual convergence” in our analysis comes from the future periods. This is because our online decision is made based on the ex-ante relaxation, while our benchmark, the LP relaxation of the offline allocation, makes the decision based on each sample path of future periods. The myopic regret caused by this distinction is shown to be bounded by the variance of the dual variable, where the randomness comes from the sample path of future periods. Though the future sample path is convoluted, we only use its distribution to give a bound and our algorithm does not require any knowledge of the realization.
Bray 2022
presents another way to derive the dual convergence bound and obtains the
O(\frac{1}{s})
bound independently.

Discussion on the second-order growth condition.
We do acknowledge that the second-order growth condition in
Section

4
is somewhat stronger than the one in existing literature
(
Li and Ye 2021
,
Balseiro et al. 2021
,
Bray 2022
)
. To be specific, the second-order growth condition assumed in
Section

4
holds for the Lagrangian dual function given
any
remaining average capacities, while the condition in the existing literature holds given remaining average capacities belonging to a neighborhood of the initial average capacities. However, the second-order growth condition in
Section

4
is a consequence of our problem formulation instead of a primitive assumption. Moreover, since it is assumed that the ex-ante relaxation admits a unique optimal dual variable in
Li and Ye 2021
,
Balseiro et al. 2021
,
Bray 2022
, their second-order growth conditions are stated as the strong-convexity of the Lagrangian dual function. In contrast, the second-order growth condition in our setting is stated as the strong-convexity after projecting every variable into the subspace that is spanned by the set of possible query sizes and we do not require the uniqueness of the optimal dual variable.

3
Policy with Log-squared Regret

In this section, we derive a log-squared bound for (
3
) under the following assumption over the distribution
F(\cdot)
.

{assumption}

We assume that for each
j\in[n]
, conditional on
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
being realized as any
\bm{a}_{j}\in\mathcal{A}
, the reward distribution of
\tilde{r}
is supported on the interval
[l_{j},u_{j}]
with a density function
f(\cdot|\bm{a}_{j})
, where
u_{j}\geq l_{j}\geq 0
, and it satisfies
f(r|\bm{a}_{j})\geq\alpha
, for a constant
\alpha>0
, for any
r\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
.

For notation simplicity, we denote by
F_{j}(\cdot)=F(\cdot|\bm{a}_{j})
and
f_{j}(\cdot)=f(\cdot|\bm{a}_{j})
.
Note that in the above
Section

3
, we allow
l_{j}=u_{j}
for a type
j
, i.e., the reward distribution for type
j
query is a point mass. In this case, we let the density be
f_{j}(r)=\infty
for
r=l_{j}=u_{j}
and any constant
\alpha
would satisfy
f_{j}(r)\geq\alpha
for
r\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
.

3.1
General Description of Our Approach

We now give a general description of our approach. We denote by
\bm{c}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
any vector of remaining capacities of the resources at the beginning of a period
t
. Then, on problem instance
I_{t}=\{(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}),\dots,(\tilde{r}_{T},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{T})\}
, we denote by
\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})
a relaxation of the total reward collected by the prophet from period
t
up to period
T
, given the remaining capacity
\bm{c}
, where the decision variable
x_{\tau}\in\{0,1\}
is relaxed into
x_{\tau}\in[0,1]
for
\tau=t,\dots,T
. We specify various formulations of the relaxation
\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})
to deal with various settings in the following sections.
Then, the regret of any online policy
\pi
can be upper bounded by the gap between
\mathbb{E}_{I_{1}\sim F}[\bar{V}_{\mathbf{C}}(I_{1})]
, where
\bm{C}=(C_{1},\dots,C_{m})
is a vector of initial capacity for all resources, and
\mathbb{E}_{\pi,I_{1}\sim F}[V^{\pi}(I)]
, i.e.,

\text{Regret}(\pi)\leq\mathbb{E}_{I_{1}\sim\bm{F}}[\bar{V}_{\mathbf{C}}(I_{1})]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi,I_{1}\sim\bm{F}}[V^{\pi}(I_{1})].

(6)

Our approach relies on the following decomposition of the upper bound in (
6
). For each
t\in[T]
, we denote by
\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}=(\tilde{c}^{\pi}_{t,1},\dots,\tilde{c}^{\pi}_{t,m})\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
the remaining capacities at the beginning of period
t
during the execution of the policy
\pi
. Note that
\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}
is random for each
t\in[T]
, where the randomness comes from the randomness in the problem instance
I
and any randomness in the policy
\pi
. Then, the term
\bar{V}_{\mathbf{C}}(I_{1})
can be telescoped as follows by noting that
\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{1}=\bm{C}
and
\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}(I_{T+1})=0
for every
\bm{c}
:

\bar{V}_{\mathbf{C}}(I_{1})=\bar{V}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{1}}(I_{1})-\bar{V}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{T+1}}(I_{t+1})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\bar{V}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})-\bar{V}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t+1}}(I_{t+1})\right).

(7)

Thus, the regret upper bound (
6
) can be decomposed as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{I_{1}\sim\bm{F}}[\bar{V}_{\mathbf{C}}(I_{1})]-\mathbb{E}_{\pi,I_{1}\sim\bm{F}}[V^{\pi}(I_{1})]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi,I_{t}\sim\bm{F}}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\bar{V}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})-\bar{V}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t+1}}(I_{t+1})-\tilde{r}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\pi,I_{t}\sim\bm{F}}\left[\bar{V}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})-\bar{V}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t+1}}(I_{t+1})-\tilde{r}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\pi,I_{t}\sim\bm{F}}\left[\bar{V}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})-\bar{V}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t+1})-\tilde{r}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}\right]

where the third equality follows from the identity that
\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t+1}=\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}
.
We proceed to analyze the term for each
t\in[T]
in the above summation.
For each
\bm{c}\geq 0
, we now denote by

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\pi,I_{t}}\left[\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})-\bar{V}_{\bm{c}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t+1})-\tilde{r}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}\right].

(8)

It is clear that in order to upper bound Regret
(\pi)
, it is sufficient to upper bound Myopic
{}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c})
for each
t\in[T]
and each
\bm{c}\geq 0
. We summarize the above arguments in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1

For any feasible online policy
\pi
, the regret is upper bounded by

\text{Regret}(\pi)\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}\left[\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi})\right]

where the myopic term
\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi})
is defined in (
8
).

We now motivate our policy
\pi
such that the myopic term Myopic
{}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c})
can be minimized for each
\bm{c}
. Now suppose that the online decision maker is allowed to “foresee” the sample path
I_{t}
and we denote by

M_{\bm{c},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})=\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t+1})-\bar{V}_{\bm{c}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})

the marginal increase for the relaxation
\bar{V}
to have an extra
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
resources from period
t+1
to
T
. Clearly, in order to minimize
\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c})
in (
8
), we set
\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}=1
if and only if

\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})-\bar{V}_{\bm{c}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})-\tilde{r}_{t}\leq\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})-\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t+1})

which implies that

\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&\text{if\penalty\ }\tilde{r}_{t}\geq M_{\bm{c},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})\\
&0,&\text{if\penalty\ }\tilde{r}_{t}<M_{\bm{c},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1}).\end{aligned}\right.

However, note that in order for
\pi
to be feasible,
\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}
must be independent of
I_{t+1}
. Therefore, instead of comparing
\tilde{r}_{t}
to the marginal increase
M_{\bm{c},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})
, we compare
\tilde{r}_{t}
to an estimator
\hat{M}_{\bm{c},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}
that is independent of
I_{t+1}
. Our policy is formalized in
Algorithm

1
, which takes as an input an exogenous estimator
\hat{M}
that we further specify in the following sections on different settings. In what follows, we first specify the relaxation
\bar{V}
that will be used in this section, and then we specify the
\hat{M}
-estimator for our algorithm and derive the corresponding regret bound.

Algorithm 1

\hat{M}
-estimator policy (
\pi_{\hat{M}}
)

1:

Input: an estimator
\hat{M}
.

2:

Initialize the initial capacities
\bm{c}_{1}=\bm{C}
.

3:

for

t=1,...,T

do

4:

Observe the value of
(\tilde{r}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t})
and obtain the value of
\hat{M}_{\bm{c}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}
.

5:

if
\tilde{r}_{t}\geq\hat{M}_{\bm{c}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}
and
\mathbf{c}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
, then we set
x_{t}=1
and update
\bm{c}_{t+1}=\bm{c}_{t}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
;

6:

otherwise, set
x_{t}=0
and update
\bm{c}_{t+1}=\bm{c}_{t}
.

7:

end

for

8:

Output: online decisions
\bm{x}=(x_{1},...,x_{T})
.

3.2
Semi-fluid Relaxation

We now specify the semi-fluid relaxation
\bar{V}
that will be used in this section. For each
j\in[n]
, we let
d_{j}
denote a generic non-negative integer that should be interpreted as
the number of type
j
query arrivals remaining, regardless of the current time period. Meanwhile, we let
\tilde{d}_{j,t}
be the random variable for the number of type
j
query arrivals from period
t
to period
T
, i.e., the number of times that
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{\tau}=\bm{a}_{j}
for
\tau=t,\dots,T
.
We introduce
\bar{V}
is as follows, for a fixed
\bm{d}=(d_{1},\dots,d_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\displaystyle\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})=

\displaystyle\max_{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{r\sim F_{j}}[r\cdot x_{j}(r)]

(9)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot a_{j,i}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{r\sim F_{j}}[x_{j}(r)]\leq c_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m]

\displaystyle x_{j}(r)\in[0,1],\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n],\forall r\in[l_{j},u_{j}].

In the following lemma, we show that the formulation of
\bar{V}
introduced in (
9
) implies an upper bound of the offline optimum
V^{\text{off}}
in (
2
).

Lemma 3.2

It holds that
\mathbb{E}_{I}[\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{C}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{1})]\geq\mathbb{E}_{I}[V^{\text{off}}(I)]
, where
\tilde{\bm{d}}_{1}=(\tilde{d}_{1,1},\dots,\tilde{d}_{n,1})
depends on the sample path
I
.

In order to see that
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{C}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{1})
is an upper bound, we first fix the type arrivals of the queries that is implied by the sample path
I
. We then take an ex-ante relaxation over the reward distribution for each type.

Comparison with other relaxations.
There are also other relaxations of the prophet (
2
) existing in the literature and we now compare
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
9
) with them.

The most natural relaxation of the prophet (
2
) is an LP relaxation, which is defined for each
t\in[T]
, any
\bm{c}\geq 0
, and any sample path
I
.

\displaystyle\bar{V}^{\text{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})=

\displaystyle\max_{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=t}^{T}\tilde{r}_{\tau}\cdot x_{\tau}

(10)

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.t.\ }

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau=t}^{T}\tilde{a}_{\tau,i}\cdot x_{\tau}\leq c_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m]

\displaystyle x_{\tau}\in[0,1]\quad\quad\forall\tau=t,\dots,T.

The only difference between the formulation of
V^{\text{off}}(I)
and
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{C}}(I_{1})
is that the integral decision variables of
V^{\text{off}}(I)
are relaxed to be fractional in
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{C}}(I_{1})
.

Another common relaxation in the literature is the so-called ex-ante relaxation, which can be obtained from replacing
\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t}
by its expectation in the formulation of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t})
. For any
t\in[T]
and any
\bm{c}
, we denote by
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{t,\bm{c}}
the ex-ante relaxation with a formulation given as follows:

\displaystyle\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{t,\bm{c}}=

\displaystyle\max_{\bm{x}}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}\cdot s\cdot\mathbb{E}_{r\sim F_{j}}[r\cdot x_{j}(r)]

(11)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}\cdot s\cdot a_{j,i}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{r\sim F_{j}}[x_{j}(r)]\leq c_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m]

\displaystyle x_{j}(r)\in[0,1],\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n],\forall r\in[l_{j},u_{j}]

where we denote by
s=T-t+1
for notation brevity.

3.3
Policy and Regret Analysis

We now develop the estimator that will be used in
Algorithm

1
and analyze the regret bound. It is easy to see that the optimal solution of (
9
) preserves a “threshold” property as formalized in the following lemma, where the proof is relegated to
Appendix

E
.

Lemma 3.3

Denote by
\{x^{*}_{j}(r),\forall j\in[n],\forall r\}
an optimal solution to (
9
). Then, there exists a set of thresholds
\{\kappa_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
such that it is optimal to set
x^{*}_{j}(r)=1
if and only if
r\geq\kappa_{j}
and
x^{*}_{j}(r)=0
if and only if
r<\kappa_{j}
, for any
j\in[n]
.

Note that Topkis’ theorem
(
Topkis 1978
)
can also be used to derive
Lemma

3.3
. Following
Lemma

3.3
,
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
in (
9
) can be re-written into the following formulation:

\displaystyle\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})=

\displaystyle\max_{\bm{q}}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot\int_{q=1-q_{j}}^{1}F_{j}^{-1}(q)dq

(12)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot a_{j,i}\cdot q_{j}\leq c_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m]

\displaystyle q_{j}\in[0,1],\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n].

Here, the decision variable
q_{j}
can be interpreted as the probability of serving type
j
query, for each
j\in[n]
. Denote by
\{\tilde{q}^{*}_{j}\}
one optimal solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t})
. Since
\{\tilde{q}^{*}_{j}\}
depends on the sample path
I_{t}
, clearly, one cannot directly use
\{\tilde{q}^{*}_{j}\}
to derive a feasible online policy that is “agnostic” about
I_{t+1}
. Therefore, we will consider using the optimal solution of the ex-ante problem (
11
)
to “approximate”
\{\tilde{q}^{*}_{j}\}
. It is clear to see that the optimal solution of the ex-ante problem (
11
) also preserves a threshold property and
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{t,\bm{c}}
(
11
) can be re-written into the following formulation:

\displaystyle\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{t,\bm{c}}=

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}\cdot s\cdot\int_{q=1-q_{j}}^{1}F_{j}^{-1}(q)dq

(13)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}\cdot s\cdot a_{j,i}\cdot q_{j}\leq c_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m]

\displaystyle q_{j}\in[0,1],\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n].

Note that the formulation of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{t,\bm{c}}
deviates from the formulation of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t})
only in that the random variable
\tilde{d}_{j,t}
is changed into its expectation
p_{j}\cdot s
. We can bound how this change of parameter would result in a change of the expected reward that we can gain from each type of query in the optimization problem (
12
) and (
13
). Our analysis generalizes the Lipschitz analysis in
Mangasarian and Shiau 1987
from linear programming to a general convex optimization problem. Our argument is formalized in the following lemma, where the proof is relegated to
Appendix

E
.

Lemma 3.4

There exists a constant
\kappa_{1}
such that for any
\bm{c}\geq 0
and any optimal solution
\hat{\bm{q}}^{*}=(\hat{q}^{*}_{j})_{j=1}^{n}
to (
13
), it holds that

\|\hat{\bm{q}}^{*}-\tilde{\bm{q}}^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{|d_{j}/s-p_{j}|\}

(14)

for any sample path
I_{t}
, where
s=T-t+1
, and
\tilde{\bm{q}}^{*}=(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j})_{j=1}^{n}
denotes one optimal solution to (
12
). Moreover, the constant
\kappa_{1}
can be set as follows,

\kappa_{1}=\max_{j\in[n]}\left\{\frac{1}{p_{j}}\right\}\cdot(m+2n)\cdot\bar{a}^{n}.

(15)

The formal policy is given in
Algorithm

2
.
We now provide the regret analysis.

Algorithm 2

Algorithm achieving
O(\log^{2}T)
Regret

1:

Input: the remaining inventory
\bm{c}
, a constant
\kappa_{1}
given in (
15
), and the type of query
t
, denoted by
j_{t}
.

2:

Obtain
\{\hat{q}^{*}_{j,t}\}
by solving the optimization problem (
13
).

3:

if

\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\geq 1-2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(T-t+1)}{T-t+1}}
, then we set
\hat{M}_{\bm{c},\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}=l_{j_{t}}
.

4:

else if

\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq 2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(T-t+1)}{T-t+1}}
, then we set
\hat{M}_{\bm{c},\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}=u_{j_{t}}+1
.

5:

else if

2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(T-t+1)}{T-t+1}}\leq\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq 1-2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(T-t+1)}{T-t+1}}
, then we set
\hat{M}_{\bm{c},\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}=F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(1-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})
.

6:

Output:
\hat{M}_{\bm{c},\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}

Theorem 3.5

Denote by
\pi

Algorithm

1
with the estimator
\hat{M}
given in
Algorithm

2
, and a constant
\kappa_{1}
defined in (
15
).
Then, it holds that

\text{Regret}(\pi)\leq\left(\frac{2\kappa_{1}+2}{\alpha}+\frac{4}{\alpha}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{p_{j}}\right)\cdot\log^{2}T+s_{0}\cdot r_{\max}.

where
r_{\max}=\max_{j\in[n]}\{u_{j}\}
denotes the upper bound of the reward, and
s_{0}
is a constant that depends on
\kappa_{1}
,
n
and
\{p_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.5
.

We have that

\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq.

(16)

where we denote by
j_{t}
the type of query
t
in the instance
I
.
Now we plug (
16
) into the formulation (
8
) where the term
\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})
is set to be
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t})
. We get

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\right.

(17)

\displaystyle\left.-\int_{q=1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq-\mathbb{E}_{r\sim F_{j_{t}}}[\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j_{t}}\cdot\tilde{x}_{t}^{\pi}(r)}(I_{t+1})]\right]

where we denote by
\pi
our online policy
Algorithm

1
with the estimator given in
Algorithm

2
. Then,
{q}^{\pi}_{j,t}
denotes the ex-ante probability that query
t
will be served by the online policy
\pi
. With these notations, (
17
) can be re-written as

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c})=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\right.

\displaystyle\left.-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}\cdot\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})-(1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t})\cdot\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})\right].

We construct feasible solutions to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
and
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
to upper bound the myopic regret. We identify a “good” event as one in which
\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}
is close to
\hat{q}^{*}_{j,t}
for each
j\in[n]
. Following
Lemma

3.4
, we know that

|\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j,t}|\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\max_{j^{\prime}}\{|p_{j^{\prime}}-\tilde{d}_{j^{\prime},t+1}/(s-1)|\}

(18)

for a constant
\kappa_{1}>0
.
We note that for each
j\in[n]
,
\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}
is a binomial distribution with mean
p_{j}\cdot(s-1)
. Then, from Hoeffding’s inequality (
Lemma

D.4
), we have

P(|\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}-p_{j}(s-1)|\leq\sqrt{(s-1)\log(s-1)})\geq 1-2\exp(-2\log(s-1))=1-\frac{2}{(s-1)^{2}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{n(s-1)}

as long as
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}\geq 2n+1
. We denote by the event

\mathcal{G}=\{|\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}-p_{j}(s-1)|\leq\sqrt{(s-1)\log(s-1)},\forall j\in[n]\},

which is the
“good”
event that
\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}
is close to its mean. From union bound, we know that

P(\mathcal{G})\geq 1-\sum_{j=1}^{n}(1-P(|\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}-p_{j}(s-1)|\leq\sqrt{(s-1)\log(s-1)}))\geq 1-\frac{1}{s-1}.

We denote by

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})=

\displaystyle P(\mathcal{G})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\right.

(19)

\displaystyle\left.-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}\cdot\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})-(1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t})\cdot\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})\mid\mathcal{G}\right]

and

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G}^{c})=

\displaystyle P(\mathcal{G}^{c})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\right.

\displaystyle\left.-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}\cdot\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})-(1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t})\cdot\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})\mid\mathcal{G}^{c}\right]

where
\mathcal{G}^{c}
is the complement of
\mathcal{G}
. It is clear that

\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c})=\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})+\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G}^{c}).

We have a direct upper bound

\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G}^{c})\leq\frac{\max_{j\in[n]}\{u_{j}\}}{s-1}.

(20)

In what follows, we condition on the event
\mathcal{G}
happening, and we bound
\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})
.

Case (i) when
\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\geq 1-2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}
:
Note that conditioning on the event
\mathcal{G}
, following (
18
), we have

|\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}|\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(s-1)}{s-1}}.

Such a case implies that
\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\geq 1-3\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\geq\frac{1}{2}
when
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}
satisfying
\frac{s_{0}}{\log s_{0}}\geq 36\kappa_{1}^{2}
. Recall that
\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}
denotes the quantile for query
t
to be accepted by the offline optimum. We know that query
t
of type
j_{t}
should also be accepted by our algorithm in a high quantile. In fact, since
\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\geq 1-O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\right)
, we can set the quantile
q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}
of our online algorithm to be any value at the order of
1-O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\right)
, and the order of the myopic regret will be bounded at the order of
O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\right)
. For simplicity, we set
q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}=1
and we summarize our results for Case (i) in the following lemma, where the proof is relegated to
Appendix

E
.

Lemma 3.6

as long as
s\geq s_{0}
for
s_{0}
satisfying
\frac{s_{0}}{\log s_{0}}\geq 16\kappa_{1}^{2}
and
s_{0}\geq 2\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{\frac{1}{p_{j}}\}
, we can obtain the following result.

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})\leq\frac{2\log s}{\alpha\cdot s}+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}]+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}}\mid\mathcal{G}\right].

(21)

Case (ii) when
\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq 2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}
:
Note that conditioning on the event
\mathcal{G}
, following (
18
), we have

|\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}|\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(s-1)}{s-1}}

which implies
\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq 3\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
as long as
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}
satisfying
\frac{s_{0}}{\log s_{0}}\geq 36\kappa_{1}^{2}
. We know that query
t
will be rejected by the offline optimum with a high probability. Therefore, we can simply set
{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}=0
such that query
t
is rejected by our online algorithm. Following such a way, we obtain the following result, where the formal proof is relegated to
Appendix

E
.

Lemma 3.7

As long as
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}
satisfying
\frac{s_{0}}{\log s_{0}}\geq 36\kappa_{1}^{2}
, we know that

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})\leq\frac{2\log s}{\alpha\cdot s}+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}]+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}\mid\mathcal{G}\right].

(22)

Case (iii) when
2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\leq\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq 1-2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}
:
Note that conditioning on the event
\mathcal{G}
, following (
18
), we have

|\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}|\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(s-1)}{s-1}}

which implies
\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\leq\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq 1-\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}
.
In this case, query
t
is accepted by the offline optimum with a probability not too close to either
0
or
1
. We mimic this point in our online algorithm by setting
q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}=\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}
and we obtain the following result, where the formal proof is relegated to
Appendix

E
.

Lemma 3.8

As long as
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}
satisfying
s_{0}\log s_{0}\geq\frac{4}{\kappa_{1}^{2}}\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{\frac{1}{p_{j}^{2}}\}
and
\frac{s_{0}}{\log s_{0}}\geq\max_{j\in[n]}\{\frac{4}{p_{j}^{2}}\}
, we have that

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})\leq\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha\cdot d_{j_{t},t+1}}+\frac{(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G}).

(23)

From (
21
), (
22
) and (
23
), for all cases, it holds that

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\log s}{\alpha\cdot s}+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}]+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}}\mid\mathcal{G}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\log s}{\alpha\cdot s}+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}]+\frac{4}{\alpha(s-1)}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{p_{j}}.

From
Lemma

3.4
, we know that there exists a constant
\kappa_{1}
with formulation given in (
15
) such that

\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}]\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[(p_{j}-\tilde{d}_{j,t}/s)^{2}]\leq\frac{\kappa_{1}}{s}.

Therefore, as long as
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}
satisfying

s_{0}\geq 2n+1,\penalty\ \frac{s_{0}}{\log s_{0}}\geq 36\kappa_{1}^{2},\penalty\ s_{0}\geq\max_{j\in[n]}\{\frac{2}{p_{j}}\},\penalty\ s_{0}\cdot\log s_{0}\geq\frac{4}{\kappa_{1}^{2}}\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{\frac{1}{p_{j}^{2}}\}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\frac{s_{0}}{\log s_{0}}\geq\max_{j\in[n]}\{\frac{4}{p_{j}^{2}}\},

we have that

\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})\leq\frac{2\log s}{\alpha\cdot s}+\frac{2\kappa_{1}}{\alpha\cdot s}+\frac{4}{\alpha(s-1)}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{p_{j}}.

Together with (
20
), we have the following regret bound over the final regret bound of our algorithm,

\text{Regret}(\pi)\leq\left(\frac{2\kappa_{1}+2}{\alpha}+\frac{4}{\alpha}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{p_{j}}\right)\cdot\log^{2}T+s_{0}\cdot r_{\max}.

Our proof is completed.

\square

4
Policy with Logarithmic Regret under Second-order Growth

In this section, we derive an improved logarithmic regret bound for (
3
) under the following stronger assumption.

{assumption}

There exists a compact convex set
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}_{\geq 0}
such that for any
t\in[T]
, any
\bm{c}
and any problem instance
I_{t}
, the relaxed offline optimum
\bar{V}^{\text{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})
(
10
) possesses one optimal dual solution
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}
satisfying
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}\in\Omega
. Moreover, there exists two positive constants
\underline{\alpha},\bar{\alpha}
such that for any
\bm{\mu}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}\in\Omega
, it holds that

\underline{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}}\left[(\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{\prime}-\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}}\left[\left(F(\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{\prime}|\tilde{\bm{a}})-F(\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime}|\tilde{\bm{a}})\right)\cdot(\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{\prime}-\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})\right]\leq\bar{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}}\left[(\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{\prime}-\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})^{2}\right]

(24)

where
\tilde{\bm{a}}
is a random variable denoting the size of one query and is realized as
\bm{a}_{j}
with probability
p_{j}
, for
j\in[n]
.

We now provide an example to illustrate under which condition
Section

4
holds or not.

Example 4.1

Consider a special case of our model where there is a single resource, i.e.,
m=1
, and two types of queries, i.e.,
n=2
. For each type
j=1\text{\penalty\ or\penalty\ }2
, the size
a_{j}=1
and the reward distribution is a uniform distribution over the interval
[l_{j},u_{j}]
with
u_{1}\leq u_{2}
. Note that
Section

4
essentially requires that
u_{1}\geq l_{2}
, i.e., the support for the reward distribution of each type of query overlaps with each other. In order to see this point, the set
\Omega
can be specified as
[l_{1},u_{2}]
and it is clear to see that (
24
) will be satisfied.
In contrast, if
u_{1}<l_{2}
, then we can see that (
24
) will be violated by setting
\mu^{\prime}=l_{2}
and
\mu^{\prime\prime}=u_{1}
. However, the results derived in
Section

3
will still apply and a
O(\log^{2}T)
regret bound can be obtained.

The only reason why we make
Section

4
is to establish a second-order growth condition of a dual function (we formalize in
Lemma

4.6
), which is a standard condition in the stochastic programming literature. Then, we apply results from the stochastic programming literature to derive our logarithmic regret bound (we formalize in
Lemma

4.9
). Note that the second-order growth condition has been assumed frequently in the previous literature (e.g.
Bray 2022
,
Li and Ye 2021
,
Balseiro et al. 2021
) in various formulations. To be specific, conditions on the density function are imposed to establish second-order growth, as in Assumption 2 in
Li and Ye 2021
.
Balseiro et al. 2021
directly assumes the strong-convexity of the dual function while
Bray 2022
assumes the Jacobian matrix of the dual function to be full rank and continuous in a neighborhood. All these conditions ensure the second-order growth condition. By deriving our results simply under
Section

4
, our contribution would be to get rid of a so-called “non-degeneracy” assumption, which concerns the “position” of the ex-ante relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
and is different from the second-order growth condition. In the following part, we briefly illustrate the “non-degeneracy” and we provide a thorough comparison between our
Section

4
and the assumptions made in previous literature in
Appendix

B
.

Comparison with assumptions made in previous literature.
Notably, a common assumption made in the previous literature
(
Balseiro et al. 2021
,
Li and Ye 2021
,
Bray 2022
)
regarding logariathmic regret with continuous reward distribution is about the “non-degeneracy” of the ex-ante relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
. The “non-degeneracy” assumption not only requires the optimal solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
to be unique, but also requires
strict complementary slackness
condition to be satisfied by
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
, i.e., for any binding resource constraint in the optimal solution of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
, the corresponding optimal dual variable must be strictly positive. In this way, the optimal basis of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
will remain unchanged if
\bm{C}
is perturbed by a certain amount, which drives all the analysis in
Balseiro et al. 2021
,
Li and Ye 2021
,
Bray 2022
. In contrast, our
Section

4
simply requires the support of the reward distribution of each type to “overlap” with each other, as shown in Example 1, and our goal of
Section

4
is to establish the standard second-order growth condition of the dual function. We require nothing over the “position” of the ex-ante relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
, including unique optimal solution condition, strict complementary slackness condition, and so on.

We now provide an example where the strict complementary slackness condition of the ex-ante relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
is violated, while our
Section

4
is still satisfied.

Example 4.2

Consider a model with 3 resources, each with an initial capacity of
T\cdot\frac{5\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon)}{2(1+6\varepsilon)}
. There are 4 types of queries, denoted by
j=1,2,3,4
. The size of each type of query is
\bm{a}_{1}=(0,1,1)
,
\bm{a}_{2}=(1,0,1)
,
\bm{a}_{3}=(1,1,0)
and
\bm{a}_{4}=(1,1,1)
. The reward distribution of types 1, 2, and 3 of the query is a uniform distribution over
[0,1]
, and the reward distribution of type 4 of the query is a uniform distribution over
[0,2]
. The arrival probability is
p_{1}=\frac{2\varepsilon}{1+6\varepsilon}
,
p_{2}=\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1+6\varepsilon}
,
p_{3}=\frac{2\varepsilon}{1+6\varepsilon}
and
p_{4}=\frac{\varepsilon}{1+6\varepsilon}
.

We can show that our
Section

4
is satisfied in
Example

4.2
(formalized in
Lemma

4.3
). On the other hand, following (
13
), the ex-ante relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}/T
can be formulated as:

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{3}p_{j}\cdot q_{j}\cdot(1-\frac{q_{j}}{2})+p_{4}\cdot q_{4}\cdot(2-q_{4})

(25)

s.t.

\displaystyle p_{2}q_{2}+p_{3}q_{3}+p_{4}q_{4}\leq\frac{5\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon)}{2(1+6\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle p_{1}q_{1}+p_{3}q_{3}+p_{4}q_{4}\leq\frac{5\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon)}{2(1+6\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle p_{1}q_{1}+p_{2}q_{2}+p_{4}q_{4}\leq\frac{5\varepsilon(1+\varepsilon)}{2(1+6\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle q_{1},q_{2},q_{3},q_{4}\in[0,1].

Denote by
\mu_{i}
the dual variable for constraint for resource
i
. Then, we can show that the primal-dual pair
\mu_{1}^{*}=\mu^{*}_{3}=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}
,
\mu^{*}_{2}=0
and
q^{*}_{1}=q^{*}_{3}=q^{*}_{4}=\frac{1+\varepsilon}{2}
,
q^{*}_{2}=\varepsilon
is optimal to (
25
) by checking that the saddle point condition is satisfied, for any
\varepsilon>0
. However, while the resource constraint is binding for every
i=1,2,3
, the optimal dual variable
\mu^{*}_{2}=0
, which shows that the
strict complementary slackness
condition is not satisfied for every
\varepsilon>0
. Therefore, the “non-degeneracy” assumption is violated for (
25
). The above argument is formalized in the following lemma, with the formal proof relegated to
Appendix

F
.

Lemma 4.3

The problem instance described in
Example

4.2
satisfies
Section

4
, while the strict complementary slackness condition is violated by the corresponding ex-ante relaxation in (
25
).

4.1
Decomposition of the Myopic Regret

We still follow the general approach described in
Section

3.1
. Following
Lemma

3.1
, we proceed to bound the myopic regret Myopic
{}_{t}(\pi,\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi})
in (
8
) under
Section

3
and
Section

4
. The difference from
Section

3
is that we now use
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})
(
10
), which is a LP relaxation of
V^{\text{off}}(I)
(
2
), to serve as the benchmark
\bar{V}
and thus provides more tractablity. Now the definition of Myopic
{}_{t}(\pi,\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi})
becomes

\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c})=\mathbb{E}_{\pi,I_{t}}\left[\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})-\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t+1})-\tilde{r}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{\pi}_{t}\right].

We denote by
\{\tilde{x}^{*}_{\tau}\}
one optimal solution of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})
(
10
), where
\tilde{x}^{*}_{\tau}\in[0,1]
for each
\tau=t,\dots,T
. Then, a gap arises from the fact that the online decision for our policy
\pi
must be binary as required by problem formulation, while the optimal solution
\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}
in the relaxation (
10
) can be fractional. In order to deal with this gap, we introduce a “rounded” relaxed offline optimum as an intermediate. To be specific, we denote by

\tilde{x}^{\text{round}}_{t}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&&\text{if\penalty\ }\tilde{r}_{t}\geq M_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})=\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi}}(I_{t+1})-\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\\
&0,&&\text{otherwise},\end{aligned}\right.

(26)

as a rounding of
\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}
.
Then, we have that

\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi}}(I_{t})=\tilde{x}^{\text{round}}_{t}\cdot(\tilde{r}_{t}+\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1}))+(1-\tilde{x}^{\text{round}})\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi}}(I_{t+1})+G_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})

(27)

where
G_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})
denotes the gap caused by rounding
\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}
to be
\tilde{x}^{\text{round}}_{t}
, which can be formulated as follows

G_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})=\tilde{r}_{t}(\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}-\tilde{x}^{\text{round}}_{t})+\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}}(I_{t+1})-\left(\tilde{x}^{\text{round}}_{t}\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1}))+(1-\tilde{x}^{\text{round}}_{t})\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi}}(I_{t+1})\right).

(28)

Introducing the rounded gap
G_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})
allows us to further decompose the regret of our policy into three terms, as formalized in the following lemma, where the proof is relegated to
Appendix

F
.

Lemma 4.4

For any
t\in[T]
, under
Section

3
and
Section

4
, it holds that

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t})\leq

\displaystyle 2\bar{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\}}\cdot\mathrm{Var}(M_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1}))]+\mathbb{E}_{I_{t}}[G_{\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi}}(I_{t})]

(29)

\displaystyle+2\bar{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\}}\cdot\left(\hat{M}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}-\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[M_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})]\right)^{2}\right]

where the variance
\mathrm{Var}
is taken over the problem instance
I_{t+1}
.

A key step in deriving
Lemma

4.4
is to utilize the relationship

\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})=\tilde{r}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}+\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\cdot\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}}(I_{t+1}).

(30)

We note that the above backward induction holds on each sample path
I
, which is the reason why we use a sample-path based relaxed offline optimum
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})
(
10
) as the benchmark. As we will show in the next section, the backward induction (
30
) enables us to reduce bounding the myopic regret into bounding the dual convergence, without requiring an additional “non-degeneracy” assumption adopted in the previous literature (we further discuss in
Appendix

B
).
This discussion reveals the benefits of considering a sample-path based benchmark
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{t,\bm{c}}(I_{t})
. The same idea of using the relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})
and relationship (
30
) has been developed in
Vera and Banerjee 2021
to get rid of the “non-degeneracy” assumption when the reward for each type is deterministic. We generalize this idea to allow the reward for each type having a continuous distribution.

For the RHS of (
29
), we refer the first term as the variation gap, the second term as the rounding gap, and the third term as the estimator gap. In what follows, we proceed to bound the three terms separately. Our goal is to show that each gap can be bounded at the order of
O(\frac{1}{T-t})
, and these bounds together will imply a
O(\log T)
bound for
Algorithm

1
.

Our analysis relies on considering the dual problem of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t+1})
and
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})
that define
M_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})
in (
8
), for each fixed
\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}
and
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
satisfying
\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
. Now for any
\bm{c}\geq 0
, we introduce a dual variable
\bm{\mu}
for the constraints of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t+1})
(
10
) and we denote by the function

\displaystyle L^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c},I_{t+1}}(\mu):

\displaystyle=\max_{\tilde{x}_{\tau}\in[0,1],\forall\tau=t+1,\dots,T}\left(\frac{\bm{c}}{s-1}\right)^{\top}\mu+\frac{1}{s-1}\cdot\sum_{\tau=t+1}^{T}[\tilde{r}_{\tau}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{\tau}^{\top}\mu]\cdot\tilde{x}_{\tau}

(31)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\bm{c}}{s-1}\right)^{\top}\mu+\frac{1}{s-1}\cdot\sum_{\tau=t+1}^{T}[\tilde{r}_{\tau}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{\tau}^{\top}\mu]^{+}.

as the dual function of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t+1})
, scaled by
\frac{1}{s-1}
, with
s=T-t+1
.
We now proceed to bound the variation gap, the rounding gap, and the estimator gap. In
Section

4.2
, we show that bounding the first two gaps can be reduced to bounding a so-called “dual convergence”, which concerns the variance of one optimal dual variable for minimizing the dual function
L^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c},I_{t+1}}(\mu)
(
31
). Then, we propose our
\hat{M}-
estimator and bound the “dual convergence” in
Section

4.3
to complete our final bound over the myopic regret.

4.2
Reduction to Dual Convergence

In this section, we show how to reduce bounding each term in (
29
) to bounding the “dual convergence”, and we also propose our
\hat{M}-
estimator.

We first bound the variation gap
\mathrm{Var}(M_{\bm{c},\bm{a}_{t}}(I_{t+1}))
for each fixed
\bm{c}
and
\bm{a}_{t}
satisfying
\bm{c}\geq\bm{a}_{t}
. We denote by

\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}\in\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in\Omega}L^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c},I_{t+1}}(\bm{\mu})\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ and\penalty\ \penalty\ }\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}\in\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in\Omega}L^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{t},I_{t+1}}(\bm{\mu}).

(32)

Note that
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
(resp.
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
) is one optimal dual variable of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t+1})
(resp.
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{t}}(I_{t+1})
). Also,
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
are random variables, where the randomness comes from the randomness of the problem instance
I_{t+1}
. The goal of introducing
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
is to lower-bound and upper-bound
M_{\bm{c},\bm{a}_{t}}(I_{t+1})
in (
8
), as in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.5

For any problem instance
I_{t+1}
and any
\bm{c}\geq\bm{a}_{t}
, it holds that

\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\mu}_{1}\leq M_{\bm{c},\bm{a}_{t}}(I_{t+1})=\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t+1})-\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{t}}(I_{t+1})\leq\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\mu}_{2}

(33)

where
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
are defined in (
32
).

We proceed to bound
\mathrm{Var}(M_{\bm{c},\bm{a}_{t}}(I_{t+1}))
with the help of
Lemma

4.5
. We note that the function
L^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c},I_{t+1}}(\bm{\mu})
can be regarded as a sample average approximation of the following stochastic optimization problem:

\min_{\bm{\mu}\in\Omega}L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{c},t+1}(\bm{\mu}):=\left(\frac{\bm{c}}{s-1}\right)^{\top}\bm{\mu}+\mathbb{E}_{(\tilde{r},\tilde{\bm{a}})\sim F}[\tilde{r}-\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}]^{+}

(34)

with
s-1=T-t
samples.
Our analysis relies on showing the second-order growth condition of the “limiting” dual function
L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{c},t+1}
defined in (
34
). Denote by
\mathcal{S}
the subspace spanned by the resource consumption vector
\bm{a}
of each query:

\mathcal{S}=\left\{\sum_{j\in[n]}\alpha_{j}\cdot\bm{a}_{j}:\forall\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

(35)

As a result of
Section

4
, we have the following second-order growth condition of the limiting dual function
L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{c},t+1}
for any
\bm{c}\geq 0
.

Lemma 4.6

For any
\bm{c}\geq 0
, we denote by
\bm{\mu}^{*}\in\argmin\penalty\ L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{c},t+1}(\bm{\mu})
such that
\bm{\mu}^{*}\in\Omega
with
\Omega
specified in
Section

4
. We also denote by
\mathcal{P}_{S}
the projection of any vector to the subspace
\mathcal{S}
(defined in (
35
)) spanned by the resource consumption vector
\bm{a}
of each query. Then for any
\bm{\mu}\in\Omega
, it holds that

L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{c},t+1}(\bm{\mu})-L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{c},t+1}(\bm{\mu}^{*})\geq\frac{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}}{2}\cdot\|\mathcal{P}_{S}(\bm{\mu}-\bm{\mu}^{*})\|_{2}^{2}

where
\underline{\beta}
denotes the smallest positive eigenvalue of
\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}}[\tilde{\bm{a}}\cdot\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}]
.

The proof is relegated to
Appendix

F
. Denote by

\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}\in\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in\Omega}L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{c},t+1}(\bm{\mu})\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ and\penalty\ \penalty\ }\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}\in\argmin_{\bm{\mu}\in\Omega}L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{t},t+1}(\bm{\mu}).

(36)

Following classical results from sample average approximation for stochastic programming
(
Shapiro 1993
)
, we know that
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
(resp.
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
) converges to
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
(resp.
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
) in probability, as
s-1=T-t\rightarrow\infty
. Thus, we can use
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
(resp.
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
) as an approximation of
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}]
(resp.
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}]
) and obtain a bound over
\mathrm{Var}(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1})
and
\mathrm{Var}(\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2})
, which finally implies an upper bound of
\mathrm{Var}(M_{\bm{c},\bm{a}_{t}}(I_{t+1}))
. We summarize the above arguments in the following lemma, which shows that we can reduce bounding the variation gap to bounding the “dual convergence” terms
\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[(\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{2}]
and
\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[(\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{2}]
. The proof is relegated to
Appendix

F
.

Lemma 4.7

Denote by
\bar{d}=\max_{j\in[n]}\{\|\bm{a}_{j}\|_{2}\}
and
\gamma=\max_{i\in[m],j\in[n]:a_{j,i}>0}\frac{u_{j}}{a_{j,i}}
. Then, for any
\bm{c}\geq 0
and any
\bm{a}_{t}
satisfying
\bm{c}\geq\bm{a}_{t}
, it holds that

\mathrm{Var}(M_{\bm{c},\bm{a}_{t}}(I_{t+1}))\leq\frac{12}{(s-1)\cdot\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}}\cdot\bar{d}^{3}\cdot m^{1/2}\cdot\gamma+14\cdot\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[(\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{2}]+14\cdot\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[(\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{2}]

with
s=T-t+1
,
where
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
are defined in (
32
),
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
are defined in (
36
), and
\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[\cdot]
denotes taking expectation over
I_{t+1}
that decides the value of
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
.

We then reduce bounding the rounding gap (second term in (
29
)) to bounding the “dual convergence”. Following previous notations, we denote by
\{\tilde{x}^{*}_{\tau}\}_{\tau=t}^{T}
an optimal solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})
(
10
). Note that
\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}\in[0,1]
can be fractional. We denote by
\tilde{x}_{t}^{\text{round}}

as the rounding of
\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}
as in (
26
). We bound the rounding gap
\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}[G_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})]
, where the formulation of
G_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})
is given in (
28
). Clearly, when
\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}=\tilde{x}_{t}^{\text{round}}
,
G_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}(I_{t})=0
. Thus, the rounding gap arises from the fact that
\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}\in[0,1]
can be different from
\tilde{x}_{t}^{\text{round}}\in\{0,1\}
.

A key observation can be summarized as follows: i) if
\bm{c}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
, then both
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
are well-defined in (
32
) and
\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}\neq\tilde{x}_{t}^{\text{round}}
happens if only
\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}_{t}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}\leq\tilde{r}_{t}\leq\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}_{t}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
; ii) if
\bm{c}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
does not hold and only
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
is well-defined in (
32
), then
\tilde{x}^{*}_{t}\neq\tilde{x}_{t}^{\text{round}}
happens if only
\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}_{t}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}\leq\tilde{r}_{t}
. Therefore, we can again involve the dual variables
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
(if well-defined) in bounding the rounding gap
G_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})
for any
\bm{c}\geq 0
