, which reduces bounding the rounding gap into bounding the “dual convergence”. We formalize the above arguments in the following lemma, where the proof is relegated to
Appendix

F
.

Lemma 4.8

For any
\bm{c}
, it holds that

\mathbb{E}_{I_{t}}\left[G_{\bm{c}}(I_{t})\right]\leq\frac{\kappa_{2}}{s-1}+\kappa_{3}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}_{t}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}_{t}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{2}]\right]+3\bar{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\bm{c}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\}}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}_{t}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}_{t}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{2}]\right]

for a constant
\kappa_{2}=\max\left\{\frac{6\bar{\alpha}\bar{d}^{3}m^{1/2}\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}},\frac{u_{\max}}{\alpha\cdot p_{\min}}\right\}
where
\bar{d}=\max_{j\in[n]}\{\|\bm{a}_{j}\|_{2}\}
and
\gamma=\max_{i\in[m],j\in[n]:a_{j,i}>0}\frac{u_{j}}{a_{j,i}}
,
u_{\max}=\max_{j}\{u_{j}\}
and
p_{\min}=\min_{j}\{p_{j}\}
, and a constant
\kappa_{3}=\max\{1,3\bar{\alpha}\}
. The variable
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
(if
\bm{c}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
and well-defined) are defined in (
32
).

4.3
Bound on Dual Convergence and Policy

In this section, we first bound the “dual convergence” and then propose our
\hat{M}-
estimator. By further utilizing the second-order growth condition established in
Lemma

4.6
, we can show the following bound over the term
\mathbb{E}[(\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{2}]
. We only state the “dual convergence” result for
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
. Since the bound is independent of
\bm{c}
, it is clear that the same bound also holds for
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
whenever well-defined (i.e.
\bm{c}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
).

Lemma 4.9

For any
\bm{c}\geq 0
, let
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
be defined in (
36
) and
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
be defined in (
32
). Then, it holds that

\mathbb{E}[(\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{2}]\leq\frac{8\bar{d}^{2}}{\underline{\alpha}^{2}\underline{\beta}^{2}\cdot(s-1)}+\frac{1}{9\bar{\alpha}\bar{d}^{2}\cdot(s-1)}+\frac{2}{s-1}

as long as
s-1=T-t\geq t_{0}
, where
t_{0}
is a constant that is determined polynomially by the problem parameters
\underline{\alpha},\underline{\beta},\bar{\alpha},\bar{d},m
.

The key of the proof of
Lemma

4.9
is to regard
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
as the solution of the sample average approximation (definition in (
32
)) of the stochastic programming (
34
) with parameter
\bm{c}
, whose optimal solution is
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
by definition (
36
). The second-order growth condition in
Lemma

4.6
enables us to apply Theorem 2.1 in
Shapiro 1993
showing that the gap between
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
is at the order of
\sqrt{\frac{1}{s-1}}
with high probability, as
s-1=T-t\rightarrow\infty
. This result is also known as asymptotic normality of sample average solution in the stochastic programming literature. Note that in order to apply Theorem 2.1 in
Shapiro 1993
, there are some additional conditions need to be satisfied. We verified that all these conditions are satisfied by our problem in the proof of
Lemma

4.9
, which is relegated to
Appendix

F
. However, the high probability result cannot be directly translated into a bound over the
L_{2}
norm. In order to obtain the bound over the
L_{2}
norm, we further utilize the method developed in
Li and Ye 2021
. As a result, a combination of the methods from
Shapiro 1993
and
Li and Ye 2021
enables us to bound the
L_{2}
norm at the order of
\frac{1}{s-1}
, which improves the
\frac{\log\log(s-1)}{s-1}
bound established in Theorem 1 in
Li and Ye 2021
.

We now present our
\hat{M}-
estimator to complete our algorithm and provide the final regret bound. From
Lemma

4.5
, we know that
M_{c,\bm{a}_{t}}(I_{t+1})
is close to
\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
and from the “dual convergence” established in
Lemma

4.9
, we know that
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
is a good “approximate” of
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
. Therefore, we use
\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
as our
\hat{M}-
estimator, which is formalized in
Algorithm

1
.

Algorithm 3

Algorithm achieving
O(\log T)
Regret

1:

Input: the remaining inventory
\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}
and size
\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}
.

2:

Obtain
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
by solving

\min_{\bm{\mu}\in\Omega}L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},t+1}(\bm{\mu}):=\left(\frac{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}}{T-t}\right)^{\top}\bm{\mu}+\mathbb{E}_{(\tilde{r},\tilde{\bm{a}})\sim F}[\tilde{r}-\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}]^{+}.

3:

Output:
\hat{M}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}=\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}
.

We provide the following regret bound for the
\hat{M}-
estimator in
Algorithm

3
.

Theorem 4.10

Suppose that the estimator
\hat{M}
is given in
Algorithm

3
and we denote by
\pi
the policy given in
Algorithm

1
. Then, it holds that

\text{Regret}(\pi)\leq C_{1}\cdot\log T+C_{2}

where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are two constants.

Proof of
Theorem

4.10
.

From
Lemma

4.4
, we have

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t})\leq

\displaystyle 2\bar{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\}}\cdot\mathrm{Var}(M_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1}))]+\mathbb{E}_{I_{t}}[G_{\tilde{\bm{c}}_{t}^{\pi}}(I_{t})]

(37)

\displaystyle+2\bar{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\}}\cdot\left(\hat{M}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}-\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[M_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})]\right)^{2}\right].

Then, from
Lemma

4.7
and
Lemma

4.8
, we know that

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t})\leq

\displaystyle\frac{24\bar{\alpha}}{(T-t)\cdot\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}}\cdot\bar{d}^{3}\cdot m^{1/2}\cdot\gamma+\frac{\kappa_{2}}{T-t}+(28\bar{\alpha}+2\bar{\alpha}\kappa_{3})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{2}]\right]

(38)

\displaystyle+(28\bar{\alpha}+6\bar{\alpha}^{2})\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{2}]\right]

\displaystyle+2\bar{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\}}\cdot\left(\hat{M}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}-\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[M_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})]\right)^{2}\right]

where
\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1},\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
are defined in (
32
) and
\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1},\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}
are defined in (
36
), with
\bm{c}=\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}
. The constant
\kappa_{2}=\max\left\{\frac{6\bar{\alpha}\bar{d}^{3}m^{1/2}\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}},\frac{u_{\max}}{\alpha\cdot p_{\min}}\right\}
where
\bar{d}=\max_{j\in[n]}\{\|\bm{a}_{j}\|_{2}\}
and
\gamma=\max_{i\in[m],j\in[n]:a_{j,i}>0}\frac{u_{j}}{a_{j,i}}
,
u_{\max}=\max_{j}\{u_{j}\}
and
p_{\min}=\min_{j}\{p_{j}\}
, and the constant
\kappa_{3}=\max\{1,3\bar{\alpha}\}
.
Moreover, from
Lemma

4.5
and the definition of
\hat{M}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}
in
Algorithm

3
, we know

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\}}\cdot\left(\hat{M}_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}-\mathbb{E}_{I_{t+1}}[M_{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t},\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}(I_{t+1})]\right)^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\}}\cdot\left((\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}])^{2}+(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}])^{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{2}]+2\cdot\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{2}]+2\cdot\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\}}\cdot(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{2}].

We apply
4
to bound the term
\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{2}]
and we get

\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{2}]\leq\frac{2}{(T-t)\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}}\cdot\bar{d}^{3}\cdot m^{1/2}\cdot\gamma.

We apply the “dual convergence” established in
Lemma

4.9
to bound
\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{2}]
and
\mathbb{E}[\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t}\geq\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\}}\cdot(\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{2}]
at the order of
O(\frac{1}{T-t})
, as long as
T-t
is large enough. To be specific, we have

\mathbb{E}[(\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{1}-\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{1})^{2}]\leq\frac{8\bar{d}^{2}}{\underline{\alpha}^{2}\underline{\beta}^{2}\cdot(T-t)}+\frac{1}{9\bar{\alpha}\bar{d}^{2}\cdot(T-t)}+\frac{2}{T-t}

and

\mathbb{E}[(\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\tilde{\bm{\mu}}_{2}-\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\mu}}_{2})^{2}]\leq\frac{8\bar{d}^{2}}{\underline{\alpha}^{2}\underline{\beta}^{2}\cdot(T-t)}+\frac{1}{9\bar{\alpha}\bar{d}^{2}\cdot(T-t)}+\frac{2}{T-t}

as long as
T-t\geq t_{0}
, where
t_{0}
is the constant specified in
Lemma

4.9
and is determined polynomially by the problem parameters
\underline{\alpha},\underline{\beta},\bar{\alpha},\bar{d},m
.
Therefore, we have

\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\tilde{\bm{c}}^{\pi}_{t})\leq\frac{C_{1}}{T-t}

as long as
T-t\geq t_{0}
. Here, the constant
C_{1}
equals the following

C_{1}=\frac{(24\bar{\alpha}+4\alpha)\bar{d}^{3}m^{1/2}\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}}+\kappa_{2}+(64\bar{\alpha}+2\bar{\alpha}\kappa_{3}+6\bar{\alpha}^{2})\cdot\left(\frac{8\bar{d}^{2}}{\underline{\alpha}^{2}\underline{\beta}^{2}}+\frac{1}{9\bar{\alpha}\bar{d}^{2}}+2\right).

(39)

The constant
\kappa_{2}=\max\left\{\frac{6\bar{\alpha}\bar{d}^{3}m^{1/2}\gamma}{\underline{\alpha}\underline{\beta}},\frac{u_{\max}}{\alpha\cdot p_{\min}}\right\}
where
\bar{d}=\max_{j\in[n]}\{\|\bm{a}_{j}\|_{2}\}
and
\gamma=\max_{i\in[m],j\in[n]:a_{j,i}>0}\frac{u_{j}}{a_{j,i}}
,
u_{\max}=\max_{j}\{u_{j}\}
and
p_{\min}=\min_{j}\{p_{j}\}
, and the constant
\kappa_{3}=\max\{1,3\bar{\alpha}\}
. As a result, the final regret bound can be bounded as

\text{Regret}(\pi)\leq C_{1}\cdot\log T+C_{2}

where the constant
C_{1}
is defined in (
39
) and the constant
C_{2}
equals

C_{2}=t_{0}=O\left(\left(mM\cdot\frac{\bar{\alpha}^{3}\bar{d}^{7}}{\underline{\alpha}^{2}\underline{\beta}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{25}{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{q}\right),

(40)

which is determined polynomially by the problem parameters
\underline{\alpha},\underline{\beta},\bar{\alpha},\bar{d},m
.

\square

Remarks.
Note that the constant terms
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
in the regret bound of
Theorem

4.10
depend polynomially on the problem parameters specified in
Section

3
and
Section

4
. In contrast, the constant terms in the regret bound of
Theorem

3.5
depend exponentially on the number of customer types, which is
n
, based on the choice of the parameter
\kappa_{1}
in (
15
), and depend polynomially on all the other parameters. Our results show that, for the general setting where only
Section

3
is satisfied, we can apply
Algorithm

1
with the estimator
\hat{M}
given in
Algorithm

2
to achieve a regret bound
O(\log^{2}T)
, where the constant term depends exponentially on the number of customer types
n
and polynomially on all the other parameters. When the additional
Section

4
is also satisfied, we can apply
Algorithm

1
with the estimator
\hat{M}
given in
Algorithm

3
and obtain an improved
O(\log T)
, where the constant term depends polynomially on all the problem parameters including
n
. The last result matches the logarithmic regret established in a series of papers (e.g.
Li and Ye 2021
,
Bray 2022
), but without the non-degeneracy assumption.

Acknowledgments.
The authors thank Rob Bray for motivations for removing the degeneracy assumption, and He Wang for bringing up the connection with price-based NRM. The authors also would like to thank Sid Banerjee for bringing references on regret lower bound to our attention. The authors thank the editors and reviewers of the journal Operations Research for the valuable comments that greatly improve our paper.

Appendix A
Numerical Performances of Our Algorithms

In this section, we conduct numerical experiments to test the empirical performances of our algorithms. To be specific, we consider an NRM problem with
m
resources and
n
customer types. For each customer type
j\in[n]
, we randomly generate its size
\bm{a}_{j}
by drawing the value of
a_{j,i}
uniformly from the interval
[0,1]
for each
i
. We let the reward for type
j
customer follow a uniform distribution
[l_{j},u_{j}]
, with
l_{j}
uniformly drawn from
[0,1]
and
u_{j}=l_{j}+\epsilon_{j}
with
\epsilon_{j}
being uniformly drawn the interval
[0,1]
, for each
j
. We set the initial capacity
C_{i}=\alpha_{i}\cdot T
for each resource
i\in[m]
, with
\alpha_{i}
being a fixed parameter, uniformly drawn from
[0,1]
. We test the performances of our
Algorithm

2
,
Algorithm

3
, and compare their performances with previous algorithms that have been developed in the literature. Note that all our algorithms resolve the ex-ante relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}
(though our benchmark is the semi-fluid relaxation). We compare the algorithm that does not resolve the ex-ante relaxation. For example, the classic fixed bid price control heuristics (FBP) proposed in
Talluri and Van Ryzin 1998
, which solves the ex-ante relaxation once to obtain the optimal dual variable
\mu^{*}
and then accepts customer
t
if and only if there are enough remaining capacities and
r_{t}\geq\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\mu^{*}
. We also compare against the dual-based policy that has been developed in a stream of literature under various setting (e.g.
Balseiro et al. 2022
,
Li et al. 2020
,
Jiang et al. 2020
), which uses an online learning algorithm to update the dual variable
\mu_{t}
at period
t
and accepts customer
t
if and only if there are enough remaining capacities and
r_{t}\geq\bm{a}_{t}^{\top}\mu_{t}
.

We refer to
\mathsf{ALG}_{2}
as the total expected reward collected by
Algorithm

2
and refer to
\mathsf{ALG}_{3}
as the total expected reward collected by
Algorithm

3
. We then denote by
\mathsf{ALG}_{\mathsf{FB}}
as the total expected reward collected by the classic fixed bid price control heuristics, and denote by
\mathsf{ALG}_{\mathsf{DU}}
as the total expected reward collected by the dual update policy. For each instance, we repeat for
K=100
times and use their average to approximate the expected reward of a policy. In
Figure

1
(a), we show how the expected reward of each policy grows with the horizon
T
, for a fixed problem instance. As we can see, both
Algorithm

2
and
Algorithm

3
performs better than the FBP
\mathsf{ALG}_{\mathsf{FB}}
and the dual update policy
\mathsf{ALG}_{\mathsf{DU}}
, especially when
T
becomes larger and larger. We also test how the performances of the policies depend on other problem parameters, for example the number of resources
M
and the number of customer types
N
. To be specific, we fix
T=100
and plot the ratios of
\mathsf{ALG}_{\mathsf{DU}}/\mathsf{ALG}_{\mathsf{FB}}
,
\mathsf{ALG}_{2}/\mathsf{ALG}_{\mathsf{FB}}
, and
\mathsf{ALG}_{3}/\mathsf{ALG}_{\mathsf{FB}}
. For the dependency over the number of resources
M
, as shown in
Figure

1
(b), the three policies, dual update policy,
Algorithm

2
, and
Algorithm

3
, perform similar to each other, though all perform much better than the fixed bid price policy. For the dependency over the number of customer types
N
, as shown in
Figure

1
(c),
Algorithm

2
and
Algorithm

3
perform better than the dual update policy when
N
becomes larger and larger. Therefore, we conclude that in all instances, dual update policy,
Algorithm

2
and
Algorithm

3
perform better than the fixed bid price policy. When the number of periods
T
becomes larger or the number of customer types
N
becomes larger,
Algorithm

2
and
Algorithm

3
perform better than the dual update policy.

(a)

The dependency of the policy rewards on
T
. X-axis denotes the value of
T
while the y-axis denotes the average reward of each policy.

(b)

The dependency of the policy rewards on the number of resources
M
. X-axis denotes the value of
M
while the y-axis denotes the ratio of the average reward of each policy over the fixed bid price policy.

(c)

The dependency of the policy rewards on the number of customer types
N
. X-axis denotes the value of
N
while the y-axis denotes the ratio of the average reward of each policy over the fixed bid price policy.

:
The comparison between the four policies, fixed bid price policy, dual update policy,
Algorithm

2
, and
Algorithm

3
.

Appendix B
Detailed Comparison to Assumptions in Existing Literature

We conclude by making comparisons between our assumptions and the assumptions made in the existing literature, which can be summarized into the following two conditions:

(i). The non-degeneracy assumption over the ex-ante relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}
, which requires the optimal solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}
to be unique and strict complementary slackness condition being satisfied.

(ii). The second-order growth condition over the dual function
L^{\mathrm{Fld}}
.

We first summarize the assumptions made in
Li and Ye 2021
in the language of our paper as follows:

{assumption}
[Assumptions made in
Li and Ye 2021
]
The following conditions have to be satisfied:

(i).
\tilde{r}_{t}
and
\|\tilde{\bm{a}}_{t}\|_{2}
are always bounded for each
t\in[T]
.

(ii).
\mathbf{C}
scales linearly in
T
and
C_{i}/T\in(\underline{d},\bar{d})
with
\bar{d}\geq\underline{d}>0
, for each
i\in[m]
.

(iii). The matrix
\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{a}}}[\tilde{\bm{a}}\tilde{\bm{a}}^{\top}]
is positive definite.

(iv). There exists a set
\Omega_{\bm{\mu}}
containing all possible optimal dual variable to the offline optimum
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Off}}
(
10
) such that for any
\bm{\mu}\in\Omega_{\bm{\mu}}
and any
\bm{c}\in\Omega_{\bm{c}}=[\underline{d}\cdot T,\bar{d}\cdot T]^{m}
, it holds that

\underline{\alpha}\cdot|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\mu}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{*}(\bm{c})|\leq|F(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\mu}|\bm{a})-F(\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{*}(\bm{c})|\bm{a})|\leq\bar{\alpha}\cdot|\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\mu}-\bm{a}^{\top}\bm{\mu}^{*}(\bm{c})|

for any
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
,
where
\bm{\mu}^{*}(\bm{c})
is the optimal dual solution to the ex-ante relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{c}}
(
11
).

(v). For any
\bm{c}\in\Omega
, the optimal dual solution
\bm{\mu}^{*}(\bm{c})
to the ex-ante relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{c}}
(
11
) satisfies the strict complementary slackness condition.

It is shown in Proposition 2 of
Li and Ye 2021
that condition (iv) in
Appendix

B
implies the second-order growth condition of the dual function, while condition (iii), (iv) and (v) all together imply the non-degeneracy assumption.
We summarize the assumptions made in
Bray 2022
as follows:

{assumption}
[Assumptions made in
Bray 2022
]
The following conditions have to be satisfied:

(i). We have
f(r|\bm{a})\leq\bar{\alpha}
for any
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
and any
r
in the support.

(ii). For any
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
,
\mathbb{E}_{(\tilde{r},\tilde{\bm{a}})\sim F}[\tilde{r}|\tilde{\bm{a}}=\bm{a}]\leq\beta
for a constant
\beta>0
.

(iii).
\|\bm{a}\|_{2}\leq\bar{d}
for any
\bm{a}\in\mathcal{A}
.

(iv). The optimal dual solution to the Lagrangian problem
\min_{\bm{\mu}\geq 0}L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{c},1}
of the ex-ante relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{c}}
(
11
) is unique and the strictly complementary slackness condition is satisfied, when
\bm{c}
belongs to a neighborhood of
\bm{C}
, which scales linearly in
T
.

(v). The Hessian matrix of
L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{C},1}(\bm{\mu}^{*})
over
\bm{\mu}^{*}
, where
\bm{\mu}^{*}=\text{argmin}_{\bm{\mu}\geq 0}L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{C},1}
is full rank (equivalently, positive definite).

(vi). The Hessian matrix of
L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{C},1}(\bm{\mu})
over
\bm{\mu}
is Lipschitz continuous when
\bm{\mu}
belongs to a neighborhood of
\bm{\mu}^{*}=\text{argmin}_{\bm{\mu}\geq 0}L^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{\bm{C},1}
.

Note that condition (v) and (vi) in
Appendix

B
together imply the second-order growth condition over the dual function, while the non-degeneracy assumption is stated in condition (iv) in
Appendix

B
.
In particular,
Balseiro et al. 2021
has summarized the assumptions in to the following two conditions:

{assumption}
[Assumption 2 in
Balseiro et al. 2021
]
The following conditions have to be satisfied:

(i). The binding constraints for
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{c}}
remains the same as the binding constraints for
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
, as long as
\bm{c}
belongs to a neighborhood of the initial capacity
\bm{C}
, where we denote by
\mathcal{J}
the set of resource constraints that are binding.

(ii). There exists a constant
\kappa>0
such that

\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{c}}-\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}\geq\left(\nabla_{\bm{C}}\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}\right)^{\top}\left(\frac{\bm{c}}{T}-\frac{\bm{C}}{T}\right)-\kappa\cdot\left(\frac{\bm{c}_{\mathcal{J}}}{T}-\frac{\bm{C}_{\mathcal{J}}}{T}\right)^{2}

for all
\bm{c}
belonging to a neighborhood of the initial capacity
\bm{C}
,
where
\bm{c}_{\mathcal{J}}
denotes
\mathcal{J}
part of the vector
\bm{c}
and
\bm{C}_{\mathcal{J}}
denotes
\mathcal{J}
part of the vector
\bm{C}
.

It has been shown that a sufficient condition to guarantee condition (i) in
Appendix

B
is that strict complementary slackness condition is satisfied by
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
(SC 8 in
Balseiro et al. 2021
), and a sufficient condition to guarantee condition (ii) in
Appendix

B
is that the second-order growth condition is satisfied by dual function
L^{\mathrm{Fld}}
(SC 7 in
Balseiro et al. 2021
).

In
Section

4
, we need the second-order growth condition, but without the non-degeneracy condition, and we derive a
O(\log T)
regret bound following our myopic regret framework. In contrast, in
Section

3
, we get rid of both the second-order growth condition and the non-degeneracy condition and consider our problem under the most general setting. Our main result is a
O(\log^{2}T)
regret bound. Both regret bounds are new in the literature.

Perturbation attempts to overcome degeneracy.
Note that in the traditional LP literature, one prevalent way to overcome degeneracy is to perturb the right-hand side of the constraints of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
by
\delta\cdot T
with
\delta
being a constant satisfying some conditions
[
Megiddo and Chandrasekaran 1989
]
. However, such a perturbation way will lead to a
\Omega(\sqrt{T})
regret for the NRM problem. To be specific, denote by
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}(\delta)
the fluid relaxation after perturbation. Then, it holds that
|\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}(\delta)-\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}|=O(\delta\cdot T)
. It has been shown that the regret of their policies with respect to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}(\delta)
scales with
\Omega(1/\delta)
(e.g. see Theorem 1 of
Balseiro et al. 2021
). Therefore, the regret of the (certainty-equivalent) policies developed in the previous literature
[
Li and Ye 2021
,
Bray 2022
,
Balseiro et al. 2021
]
with respect to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{1,\bm{C}}
scales with
O(\delta\cdot T+1/\delta)
. From the above discussion, we conclude that the perturbation attempts to overcome degeneracy can only lead to a
O(\sqrt{T})
regret bound for the NRM problem.

Appendix C
Capturing the Price-based NRM Problem

In this
appendix
we explain how our regret results for NRM with accept/reject decisions (often called the
quantity-based
NRM problem) extend to the
price-based
NRM problem, and why our model (with a finite number of possible demand vectors but an infinite number of possible reward values) is quite natural for the pricing problem.

In the prototypical price-based NRM problem, a firm starts with initial resource vector
\bm{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
The firm is selling a finite set of products, indexed
j=1,\ldots,n
, with each product
j
needing to consume resource vector
\bm{a}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
in order to be sold. At each time
t=1,\ldots,T
, the firm must first post a price
P^{t}_{j}
for each product
j
(
P^{t}_{j}
can be
\infty
if there are insufficient resources for product
j
at time
t
). Afterward, customer
t
arrives, wanting a random product
\tilde{\jmath}_{t}
drawn independently according to a known probability vector
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})
. Conditional on the customer wanting product
j
, their valuation
\tilde{v}_{t}
is drawn independently from a known,
j
-specific distribution
H_{j}
that is continuous over an interval
[l_{j},u_{j}]
with PDF
h_{j}
satisfying
h_{j}(v)>0
.
Customer
t
makes a purchase if and only if
\tilde{v}_{t}\geq P^{t}_{\tilde{\jmath}_{t}}
, in which case the firm collects revenue
P^{t}_{\tilde{\jmath}_{t}}
and consumes resources
\bm{a}_{\tilde{\jmath}_{t}}
.
The firm’s objective is to maximize the expected total revenue collected.

Definition C.1

For all
j=1,\ldots,n
, the virtual valuation corresponding to a valuation
v
drawn from
H_{j}
is
\phi_{j}(v):=v-\frac{1-H_{j}(v)}{h_{j}(v)}
.
The expected virtual surplus is defined to be

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{S\subseteq[T]:\sum_{t\in S}\bm{a}_{\tilde{\jmath}_{t}}\leq\bm{C}}\phi_{\tilde{\jmath}_{t}}(\tilde{v}_{t})\right].

For all
j
, let
F_{j}
denote the CDF of the random variable
\phi_{j}(\tilde{v})
when
\tilde{v}
is drawn from
H_{j}
.

The virtual valuation
\phi_{j}(v)
is well-defined for all
v\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
, by the assumption that
h_{j}(v)>0
.
We now make the standard
regularity
assumption that the virtual valuation function
\phi_{j}
is monotonic, along with an assumption on
F_{j}
having a lower-bounded PDF which will allow us to apply our results by considering accept/reject decisions on rewards drawn from the distribution
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
.

{assumption}

For all
j=1,\ldots,n
, valuation distribution
H_{j}
is such that
\phi_{j}
is a non-decreasing function over
[l_{j},u_{j}]
.
Moreover, the virtual valuation distribution
F_{j}
has a PDF
f_{j}
satisfying
f_{j}(r)\geq\alpha
for all values
r\geq 0
lying in the support of
f_{j}
, where
\alpha>0
is a constant.

We note that even under the first part of
Appendix

C
(regularity), virtual valuations can be negative. The second part of
Appendix

C
imposes that the non-negative part of the support of virtual valuations for any
j=1,\ldots,n
has a PDF that is lower-bounded by
\alpha
.

Theorem C.2

(
Myerson 1981
,
Chawla et al. 2010
)

Under
Appendix

C
, the expected revenue of the optimal Bayesian incentive-compatible and individually-rational mechanism, which is an upper bound on the revenue of any online pricing policy, is equal to the expected virtual surplus.
Moreover, consider the quantity-based NRM problem on rewards drawn from the virtual valuation distributions
F_{1},\ldots,F_{n}
.
Any online accept/reject policy for this problem can be converted into an online pricing policy for the price-based NRM problem, such that
the expected revenue earned in the price-based NRM problem
equals
the expected reward collected in the quantity-based NRM problem.

Theorem

C.2
shows that regret guarantees for quantity-based NRM imply the same guarantees for price-based NRM. We note that “regret” in the price-based NRM setting is defined against the optimal mechanism benchmark, which is an upper bound on the revenue of any online pricing policy because these are special cases of sequential mechanisms.

When applying the accept/reject policy from the virtual valuation space on the original price-based NRM problem, for any
j=1,\ldots,n
, one takes the minimum acceptable virtual valuation threshold
Q_{j}
and maps it to a price
P_{j}\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
with the same probability of sale (i.e.
1-F_{j}(Q_{j})=1-H_{j}(P_{j})
) and the same immediate expected reward/revenue (i.e.
\int_{F_{j}(Q_{j})}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq=P_{j}(1-H_{j}(P_{j}))
).
Since virtual valuations can be negative, the accept/reject problem can face negative rewards, something not captured by our initial quantity-based NRM model.
However, this is easily assuaged by splitting each “type”
j
into two, one of which has a reward that is deterministically 0 (such rewards would never be accepted, so it is without loss of generality to convert negative virtual valuations into 0 rewards).

We now show that
Appendix

C
is satisfied for many valuation distributions commonly used in the pricing literature, leading to the following corollary.

Corollary C.3

If all valuation distributions satisfy
Appendix

C
, then logarithmic-level regret can be achieved for the price-based NRM problem.
In particular, the following classes of valuation distributions satisfy
Appendix

C
:

1.

Uniform valuations, i.e.
h_{j}(v)=\frac{1}{u_{j}-l_{j}}
for all
v\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
;

2.

Truncated normal valuations, i.e.
h_{j}(v)=\alpha_{j}\cdot\exp(-v^{2}/\sigma_{j})
for all
v\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
;

3.

Truncated exponential valuations, i.e.
h_{j}(v)=\alpha_{j}\cdot\exp(-\lambda_{j}\cdot v)
for all
v\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
;

Proof.

1.

Virtual valuations, defined by
\phi_{j}(v)=2v-u_{j}
for all
v\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
, are uniformly distributed over
[2l_{j}-u_{j},u_{j}]
, i.e.
f_{j}(r)=\frac{1}{2(u_{j}-l_{j})}
for all
r\in[2l_{j}-u_{j},u_{j}]
;

2.

Virtual valuations of the truncated normal valuations are known to be monotonic
[
Hartline 2013
]
. We only prove the bounds for the pdf
f_{j}(r)
. For a
v_{1},v_{2}\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
, we denote by

r_{1}=\phi_{j}(v_{1})=v_{1}-\frac{1-H_{j}(v_{1})}{h_{j}(v_{1})}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }r_{2}=\phi_{j}(v_{2})=v_{2}-\frac{1-H_{j}(v_{2})}{h_{j}(v_{2})}.

Then, it holds that

\displaystyle f_{j}(r_{1})

\displaystyle=\lim_{r_{2}\rightarrow r_{1}}\frac{F_{j}(r_{2})-F_{j}(r_{1})}{r_{2}-r_{1}}=\lim_{v_{2}\rightarrow v_{1}}\frac{H_{j}(v_{2})-H_{j}(v_{1})}{\phi^{\prime}_{j}(v_{1})\cdot(v_{2}-v_{1})}=\frac{h_{j}(v_{1})}{\phi^{\prime}_{j}(v_{1})}

(41)

\displaystyle=\frac{h^{3}_{j}(v_{1})}{2h^{2}_{j}(v_{1})+h^{\prime}_{j}(v_{1})-H_{j}(v_{1})\cdot h^{\prime}_{j}(v_{1})}.

By noting that
h_{j}^{\prime}(v_{1})\leq 0
, it is easy to see that

\frac{h_{j}(v_{1})}{2}\leq f_{j}(r_{1}).

Clearly,
\frac{h_{j}(v_{1})}{2}
is lower bounded by a positive constant on the interval
v_{1}\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
, which implies that the pdf of the virtual valuation has a positive lower bound.

3.

Virtual valuations of the truncated exponential valuations are known to be monotonic
[
Hartline 2013
]
. We only prove the bounds for the pdf
f_{j}(r)
. Following (
41
) and
h^{\prime}_{j}(v_{1})\leq 0
, we have

\frac{h_{j}(v_{1})}{2}\leq f_{j}(r_{1}).

Therefore,
f_{j}(r_{1})
is lower bounded by a positive constant for any
r_{1}
by noting that
h_{j}(v_{1})
is lower bounded by a positive constant for any
v_{1}\in[l_{j},u_{j}]
.

\square

Appendix D
Useful Known Results

We proceed now to establish Lipschitz continuity of solutions of linear systems with respect to right-hand side perturbations.

Lemma D.1

(Theorem 2.2 of
Mangasarian and Shiau 1987
)

Consider two linear systems

\hat{A}\bm{x}\leq\bm{b}^{1}

(42)

and

\hat{A}\bm{x}\leq\bm{b}^{2}.

(43)

For any solution
\bm{x}^{1}
that satisfies linear system (
42
), there exists a solution
\bm{x}^{2}
that satisfies linear system (
43
) such that

\|\bm{x}^{1}-\bm{x}^{2}\|_{\infty}\leq\mu\cdot\|\bm{b}^{1}-\bm{b}^{2}\|_{\infty}

where

\mu=\sup_{\bm{v}}\left\{\left\|\bm{v}\right\|_{1}\left|\begin{aligned} &\|\bm{v}^{\top}\hat{A}\|_{1}=1,\penalty\ \bm{v}\geq 0\\
&\text{Rows of }\hat{A}\text{ corresponding non-zero elements}\\
&\text{of }\bm{v}\text{ are linear independent.}\end{aligned}\right.\right\}.

We state the Hadamard’s inequality on matrix determinant in the following lemma.

Lemma D.2

(
Hadamard 1893
)

For any matrix
A
, let
K
denotes the number of columns in matrix
A
and let
\{\bm{a}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K}
denotes all the columns of matrix
A
. Then, the determinant of matrix
A
, denoted by
\text{det}(A)
, satisfies the following inequality

\text{det}(A)\leq\prod_{k=1}^{K}\|\bm{a}_{k}\|_{2}.

We state the well-known Bernstein’s inequality in the following lemma.

Lemma D.3

(Bernstein’s Inequality)

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{K}
be independent zero-mean random variables. Suppose that
|X_{k}|\leq M
almost surely for all
k\in[K]
. Then, for all positive
\epsilon>0
, it holds that

P\left(\frac{1}{K}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{K}X_{k}\geq\epsilon\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\frac{1}{2}\cdot K^{2}\epsilon^{2}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\text{Var}(X_{k})+\frac{1}{3}\cdot MK\epsilon}\right)

We also state the well-known Hoeffding’s inequality in the following lemma.

Lemma D.4

(Hoeffding’s Inequality)

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{K}
be independent random variables such that
a_{k}\leq X_{k}\leq b_{k}
almost surely, for each
k\in[K]
. Denote by
S_{K}=\frac{1}{K}\cdot\sum_{k=1}^{K}X_{k}
. Then, for any
\epsilon>0
, it holds that

P\left(|S_{k}-\mathbb{E}[S_{k}]|\geq\epsilon\right)\leq 2\exp(-\frac{2K^{2}\epsilon^{2}}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}(b_{k}-a_{k})^{2}}).

We then state the results from
Huber 1967
under our notations.

{assumption}

Suppose the following conditions hold:

(N-1). For each fixed
\bm{\mu}
, the function
\psi((r,\bm{a}),\bm{\mu})
is separable.

(N-2). Denote
\lambda(\bm{\mu})=\mathbb{E}_{(r,\bm{a})}[\psi((r,\bm{a}),\bm{\mu})]
, then we have
\lambda(\bm{\mu}^{*})=0
.

Denote

u((r,\bm{a}),\bm{\mu},d)=\sup_{\|\bm{\mu}^{\prime}-\bm{\mu}\|_{2}\leq d}|\psi((r,\bm{a}),\bm{\mu}^{\prime})-\psi((r,\bm{a}),\bm{\mu})|.

(N-3). There are strictly positive numbers
a,b,c_{1},c_{2},d_{0}
such that

(i).
|\lambda(\bm{\mu})|\geq a\cdot\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\mu}^{*}\|
for
\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\mu}^{*}\|\leq d_{0}
.

(ii).
\mathbb{E}_{(\tilde{r},\tilde{\bm{a}})}[u((\tilde{r},\tilde{\bm{a}}),\bm{\mu},d)]\leq b\cdot d
for
\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\mu}^{*}\|+d\leq d_{0}
.

(iii).
\mathbb{E}_{(\tilde{r},\tilde{\bm{a}})}[u((\tilde{r},\tilde{\bm{a}}),\bm{\mu},d)^{2}]\leq c_{1}\cdot d
for
\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\mu}^{*}\|+d\leq d_{0}
.

(iv).
u((r,\bm{a}),\bm{\mu},d)\leq c_{2}
for any
(r,\bm{a})
.

Denote by

Z_{n}(\bm{\mu}^{\prime},\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}[\psi((r_{j},\bm{a}_{j}),\bm{\mu}^{\prime})-\psi((r_{j},\bm{a}_{j}),\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})+\lambda(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})-\lambda(\bm{\mu}^{\prime\prime})]}{\sqrt{n}+n|\lambda(\bm{\mu}^{\prime})|}.

Then we have the following result from
Huber 1967
. Note that the original statement in
Huber 1967
only concerns the convergence of
\sup_{\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\mu}^{*}\|\leq d_{0}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{*})
to
0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. We now specify the constant terms in their bound and characterize the convergence rate, which will be helpful in our other proofs. The proof simply follows the proof in
Huber 1967
, except that we make specific the constant terms, and we include here for completeness.

Lemma D.5

(Lemma 3 in
Huber 1967
)

The conditions in
Appendix

D
imply that

\sup_{\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\mu}^{*}\|\leq d_{0}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{*})\rightarrow 0

in probability as
n\rightarrow\infty
. Moreover, for any
\epsilon>0
, it holds that

P\left(\sup_{\|\bm{\mu}-\bm{\mu}^{*}\|\leq d_{0}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\mu}^{*})\geq 2\epsilon\right)\leq c_{1}\epsilon^{-2}n^{-\gamma^{\prime}}+2\exp\left(-\frac{\min\{b^{2}q,\varepsilon^{2}a^{2}\}\cdot(1-q)^{2}n^{1-\gamma^{\prime}}}{c_{1}(3-q)+2c_{2}(b+\varepsilon a)(1-q)/3}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(\log n+\log d_{0})}{|\log(1-q)|}+1\right)\cdot(2M)^{m}

as long as
n\geq n_{0}
, where
n_{0}
satisfying
n_{0}^{\gamma^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{2b}{\epsilon}
and
\gamma^{\prime}\in(\frac{1}{2},1)
is an arbitrary number. Moreover, we set
M\geq(3b)/(\epsilon a)
and
q=1/M
.

Proof of
Lemma

D.5
.

For the sake of simplicity, and without loss of generality, we choose the coordinate system such that
\bm{\mu}^{*}=0
. We also use
\bm{x}_{j}
to denote
(r_{j},\bm{a}_{j})
for each
j\in[n]
. The idea of the proof is to divide the cube
\|\bm{\mu}\|\leq d_{0}
into a slowly increasing number of smaller cubes and to bound
Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},0)
in probability on each of those smaller cubes.

Put
q=1/M
, where
M\geq 2
is an integer to be chosen later, and consider the concentric cubes

C_{k}=\{\bm{\mu}:\|\bm{\mu}\|\leq(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}\},\penalty\ \penalty\ k=0,1,\dots,k_{0}.

Subdivide the difference
C_{k-1}\backslash C_{k}
into smaller cubes with edges of length
2d=(1-q)^{k-1}q\cdot d_{0}
such that the coordinates of their centers
\bm{\xi}
are odd multiples of
d
, and

|\bm{\xi}|=(1-q)^{k-1}(1-\frac{q}{2})\cdot d_{0}.

For each value of
k
there are less than
(2M)^{m}
such smalle cubes, so there are
N<k_{0}\cdot(2M)^{m}
cubes contained in
C_{0}\backslash C_{k_{0}}
; number them
C_{(1)},\dots,C_{(N)}
.

Now let
\epsilon>0
be given. We shall show that for a proper choice of
M
and of
k_{0}=k_{0}(n)
, the right-hand side of

P\left(\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in C_{0}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},0)\geq 2\epsilon\right)\leq P\left(\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in C_{k_{0}}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},0)\geq 2\epsilon\right)+\sum_{l=1}^{N}P\left(\sup_{\mu\in C_{(l)}}Z_{n}(\mu,0)\geq 2\epsilon\right)

(44)

tends to
0
with increasing
n
, which establishes the final result.

Actually, we shall choose

M\geq(3b)/(\epsilon a),\text{\penalty\ which\penalty\ implies\penalty\ }q\leq(\epsilon a)/(3b),

(45)

and
k_{0}=k_{0}(n)
is defined by

(1-q)^{k_{0}}\cdot d_{0}\leq n^{-\gamma^{\prime}}<(1-q)^{k_{0}-1}\cdot d_{0}

(46)

where
\frac{1}{2}<\gamma^{\prime}<1
is an arbitrary fixed number. Thus, we have

k_{0}(n)-1<\frac{\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(\log n+\log d_{0})}{|\log(1-q)|}\leq k_{0}(n),

(47)

hence

N\leq(\frac{\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(\log n+\log d_{0})}{|\log(1-q)|}+1)\cdot(2M)^{m}.

(48)

Now take any of the cubes
C_{(l)}
, with center
\bm{\xi}
and edges of length
2d
according to
2d=(1-q)^{k-1}q\cdot d_{0}
and
|\bm{\xi}|=(1-q)^{k-1}(1-\frac{q}{2})\cdot d_{0}
. For
\bm{\mu}\in C_{(l)}
, we have then by (N-3),

|\lambda(\bm{\mu})|\geq a\cdot\|\bm{\mu}\|\geq a\cdot(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}

(49)

and

|\lambda(\bm{\mu})-\lambda(\bm{\xi})|\leq\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi},d)]\leq bd\leq b(1-q)^{k}q\cdot d_{0}.

(50)

We have

Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},0)\leq Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},\bm{\xi})+\frac{\left|\sum_{j=1}^{n}[\psi(\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\xi})-\psi(\bm{x}_{j},0)-\lambda(\bm{\xi})]\right|}{\sqrt{n}+n|\lambda(\bm{\mu})|},

(51)

hence

\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in C_{(l)}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},0)\leq U_{n}+V_{n}

(52)

with

U_{n}=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}[u(\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\xi},d)+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi},d)]]}{na(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}},

(53)

and

V_{n}=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}[\psi(\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\xi})-\psi(\bm{x}_{j},0)-\lambda(\bm{\xi})]}{na(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}}.

(54)

Thus,

P(U_{n}\geq\epsilon)=P\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}[u(\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\xi},d)-\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi},d)]]\geq\epsilon na(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}-2n\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi},d)]\right).

(55)

In view of (
50
) and (
45
),

\epsilon a(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}-2\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi},d)]\geq\epsilon a(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}-2bq(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}\geq bq(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}.

(56)

Then, we know that

P(U_{n}\geq\epsilon)\leq P\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}[u(\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\xi},d)-\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi},d)]]\geq nbq(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}\right).

(57)

We now apply Bernstein’s inequality (
Lemma

D.3
) to bound the right hand side of (
57
). From condition (N-3) (iii) we know that
\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\mu},d)^{2}]\leq c_{1}\cdot d=c_{1}(1-q)^{k-1}qd_{0}/2
. From condition (N-3) (iv) we know that
|u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\mu},d)|\leq c_{2}
almost surely. Therefore, we have the following bound as the results of Bernstein’s inequality (
Lemma

D.3
).

\displaystyle P(U_{n}\geq\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}[u(\bm{x}_{j},\bm{\xi},d)-\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi},d)]]\geq nbq(1-q)^{k}\cdot d_{0}\right)

(58)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{b^{2}q^{2}(1-q)^{2k}d_{0}^{2}n^{2}}{c_{1}n(1-q)^{k-1}qd_{0}+2c_{2}nbq(1-q)^{k}d_{0}/3}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{b^{2}q^{2}(1-q)^{k+1}d_{0}n}{c_{1}q+2c_{2}bq(1-q)/3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{b^{2}q^{2}(1-q)^{k_{0}+1}d_{0}n}{c_{1}q+2c_{2}bq(1-q)/3}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{b^{2}q(1-q)^{2}n^{1-\gamma^{\prime}}}{c_{1}+2c_{2}b(1-q)/3}\right),

where the last inequality follows from (
46
) that
d_{0}(1-q)^{k_{0}+1}\geq n^{-\gamma^{\prime}}\cdot(1-q)^{2}
.

In a similar way, we apply the Bernstein inequality to bound
P(V_{n}\geq\varepsilon)
. We note that

\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[(\psi(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi})-\psi(\tilde{\bm{x}},0))^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{x}}[(u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi},|\bm{\xi}|))^{2}]\leq c_{1}\cdot|\bm{\xi}|=c_{1}(1-q)^{k-1}(1-\frac{q}{2})\cdot d_{0},

where the last inequality follows from the condition (N-3) (iii). Also, from the condition (N-3) (iv) we have that

|\psi(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi})-\psi(\tilde{\bm{x}},0)|\leq u(\tilde{\bm{x}},\bm{\xi},|\bm{\xi}|)\leq c_{2}.

Therefore, from Bernstein’s inequality (
Lemma

D.3
),
we have

\displaystyle P(V_{n}\geq\epsilon)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}a^{2}n(1-q)^{k+1}d_{0}}{c_{1}(2-q)+2c_{2}\varepsilon a(1-q)/3}\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}a^{2}n(1-q)^{k_{0}+1}d_{0}}{c_{1}(2-q)+2c_{2}\varepsilon a(1-q)/3}\right)

(59)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}a^{2}(1-q)^{2}n^{1-\gamma^{\prime}}}{c_{1}(2-q)+2c_{2}\varepsilon a(1-q)/3}\right),

where the last inequality follows from (
46
) that
d_{0}(1-q)^{k_{0}+1}\geq n^{-\gamma^{\prime}}\cdot(1-q)^{2}
.
Hence, we obtain from (
46
), (
52
), (
58
) and (
59
) that

\displaystyle P\left(\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in C_{(j)}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},0)\geq 2\epsilon\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{b^{2}q(1-q)^{2}n^{1-\gamma^{\prime}}}{c_{1}+2c_{2}b(1-q)/3}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{\varepsilon^{2}a^{2}(1-q)^{2}n^{1-\gamma^{\prime}}}{c_{1}(2-q)+2c_{2}\varepsilon a(1-q)/3}\right)

(60)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{\min\{b^{2}q,\varepsilon^{2}a^{2}\}\cdot(1-q)^{2}n^{1-\gamma^{\prime}}}{c_{1}(3-q)+2c_{2}(b+\varepsilon a)(1-q)/3}\right).

Furthermore,

\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in C_{k_{0}}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},0)\leq\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}[u(\bm{x}_{j},0,d)+\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},0,d)]]}{\sqrt{n}}

(61)

with
d=(1-q)^{k_{0}}\cdot d_{0}\leq n^{-\gamma^{\prime}}
. Hence,

P\left(\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in C_{k_{0}}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},0)\geq 2\epsilon\right)\leq P\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}[u(\bm{x}_{j},0,d)-\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},0,d)]]\geq 2\sqrt{n}\epsilon-2n\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},0,d)]\right).

(62)

Since
\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},0,d)]\leq bd\leq bn^{-\gamma^{\prime}}
, set
n_{0}
such that
n_{0}^{\gamma^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{2b}{\epsilon}
. Then, for
n\geq n_{0}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},0,d)]\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{n}}\Rightarrow 2\sqrt{n}\epsilon-2n\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\bm{x}}}[u(\tilde{\bm{x}},0,d)]\geq\sqrt{n}\epsilon;

thus, by Chebyshev’s inequality,

P\left(\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in C_{k_{0}}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},0)\geq 2\epsilon\right)\leq c_{1}\cdot\epsilon^{-2}\cdot n^{-\gamma^{\prime}}.

(63)

Now, putting (
44
), (
48
), (
60
), and (
63
) together, we obtain

P\left(\sup_{\bm{\mu}\in C_{0}}Z_{n}(\bm{\mu},0)\geq 2\epsilon\right)\leq c_{1}\epsilon^{-2}n^{-\gamma^{\prime}}+2\exp\left(-\frac{\min\{b^{2}q,\varepsilon^{2}a^{2}\}\cdot(1-q)^{2}n^{1-\gamma^{\prime}}}{c_{1}(3-q)+2c_{2}(b+\varepsilon a)(1-q)/3}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(\log n+\log d_{0})}{|\log(1-q)|}+1\right)\cdot(2M)^{m}

as long as
n\geq n_{0}
where
n_{0}
satisfying
n_{0}^{\gamma^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}}=\frac{2b}{\epsilon}
,
which completes our proof of the lemma.

\square

Appendix E
Missing Proofs for
Section

3

Proof of
Lemma

3.3
.

We now fix an arbitrary
j\in[n]
. Suppose that there exists two points
r_{1}\geq r_{2}
such that
x^{*}_{j}(r_{1})<1
while
x^{*}_{j}(r_{2})>0
. Then, denote by
\delta=\min\{1-x^{*}_{j}(r_{1}),x^{*}_{j}(r_{2})\}
and we define a new set of solution

\hat{x}^{*}_{j}(r_{1})=x^{*}_{j}(r_{1})+\delta,\penalty\ \hat{x}^{*}_{j}(r_{2})=x^{*}_{j}(r_{2})-\delta,\penalty\ \text{and\penalty\ }\hat{x}^{*}_{j^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})=x^{*}_{j^{\prime}}(r^{\prime}),\penalty\ \forall r^{\prime}\neq r_{1},r_{2},\forall j^{\prime}\neq j.

It is easy to see that
\{\hat{x}^{*}_{j}(r),\forall j\in[n],\forall r\}
is still a feasible solution to (
9
). However, the objective value under the solution
\{\hat{x}^{*}_{j}(r),\forall j\in[n],\forall r\}
can only become larger in that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{r\sim F_{j}}[r\cdot\hat{x}^{*}_{j}(r)]\geq\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{r\sim F_{j}}[r\cdot x^{*}_{j}(r)].

Therefore, we conclude that
\{\hat{x}^{*}_{j}(r),\forall j\in[n],\forall r\}
is still an optimal solution to (
9
). Keep operating as above, we can transfer any optimal solution that does not possess the threshold property into an optimal solution that enjoys the threshold property, as described in the statement of
Lemma

3.3
. Our proof is thus completed.

\square

Proof of
Lemma

3.4
.

Our proof can be classifed into three steps. We fix
\{\hat{q}^{*}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
as an optimal solution to (
13
). In the first step,
we discretize both the convex optimization problem (
12
) and (
13
) into two LPs with a granularity
K
such that
\{\hat{q}^{*}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
is an optimal solution to the discretized LP of (
13
). In the second step, we show that we can select one optimal solution to the discretized LP of (
12
), such that the gap between the selected optimal solution and
\{\hat{q}^{*}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
can be bounded by a constant independent of the granularity
K
. In the final step, we show that as the granularity
K
grows to infinity, there exists a subsequence of
K
such that the selected optimal solution to the discretized LP of (
12
) will converge to an optimal solution of (
12
), which completes our proof.

We now do the first step to discretize the convex optimization problem (
12
) and (
13
). For each
j\in[n]
, we denote by a function

G_{j}(q)=\int_{q^{\prime}=1-q}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q^{\prime})dq^{\prime}.

For any integer
K\geq n+1
, we denote by a set
\{0,\frac{1}{K-n},\frac{2}{K-n},\dots,1\}\cup\{\hat{q}^{*}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and let
q^{K}_{k}
to be the
k
-th smallest element in this set, for
k=1,2,\dots,K+1
. For each
j\in[n]
, we denote by
\hat{G}^{K}_{j}(\cdot)
the piece-wise linear interpolation of
G_{j}
based on the values at points
\{q^{K}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K+1}
. From the concavity of the function
G_{j}(\cdot)
, it is clear that we have

\hat{G}^{K}_{j}(q)\leq G_{j}(q)\leq\hat{G}^{K}_{j}(q)+\frac{\max_{j\in[n]}\{u_{j}\}}{K-n},\penalty\ \forall q\in[0,1],\penalty\ \text{and\penalty\ }\hat{G}^{K}_{j}(\hat{q}^{*}_{j})=G_{j}(\hat{q}^{*}_{j}).

(64)

Then, we know that
\{\hat{q}^{*}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
is an optimal solution to the following optimization problem

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}=

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}\cdot s\cdot\hat{G}^{K}_{j}(q_{j})

(65)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}\cdot s\cdot a_{j,i}\cdot q_{j}\leq c_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m]

\displaystyle q_{j}\in[0,1],\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n]

because optimization problem (
65
) differs from (
13
) only by having a pointwise-dominated objective function, and
\{\hat{q}^{*}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
attains the optimal objective value from (
13
) in the dominated problem (
65
).
Without loss of generality, we assume that for each
j\in[n]
, the piece-wise linear functions
\hat{G}^{K}_{j}(\cdot)
share the same set of end points of the piece-wise linear intervals, and we denote by
\{q^{K}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{K+1}
the set of end points, with
q^{K}_{1}=0
and
q^{K}_{K+1}=1
. Then, we have the following linear programming as a re-formulation of the discretization
\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}
of the convex problem
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{t,\bm{c}}
(
13
):

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}=

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{j,k}\cdot x^{K}_{k,j}

(66)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{j,i}\cdot x^{K}_{k,j}\leq c_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m]

\displaystyle 0\leq x^{K}_{k,j}\leq p_{j}\cdot s\cdot(q^{K}_{k+1}-q^{K}_{k}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n],\forall k\in[K]

where
\beta_{j,k}
is the coefficient that is inherited from the piece-wise linear function
\hat{G}^{K}_{j}
.
Here, the variable
x^{K}_{k,j}
can be interpreted as the number of queries,with type
j
and reward realization quantile lying in the interval
[q^{K}_{k},q^{K}_{k+1}]
, being served in the relaxation
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Fld}}_{t,\bm{c}}
(
11
). We also denote by the following linear programming as a discretization of the convex problem
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
12
), for each
\bm{d}
:

\displaystyle\bar{V}^{K}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})=

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\beta_{j,k}\cdot x^{K}_{k,j}

(67)

s.t.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K}a_{j,i}\cdot x^{K}_{k,j}\leq c_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m]

\displaystyle 0\leq x^{K}_{k,j}\leq d_{j}\cdot(q^{K}_{k+1}-q^{K}_{k}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n],\forall k\in[K].

We now do the second step and compare one optimal solution of
\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}
to another optimal solution of
\bar{V}^{K}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
. Our result is formalized in the following claim. Our analysis follows the analysis in
Mangasarian and Shiau 1987
over the Lipschitz continuity of solutions of linear programming, and we further show an equivalence between two linear systems to obtain a bound that is independent of the granularity
K
.

Claim 1

There exists a constant
\mu
such that for any
K
, for any optimal solution
\{\hat{x}^{K}_{k,j}\}
of
\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}
(
66
), we can select one optimal solution
\{\bar{x}^{K}_{k,j}\}
of
\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}^{K}(\bm{d})
(
67
) such that

\left|\sum_{k=1}^{K}\hat{x}^{K}_{k,j}-\bar{x}^{K}_{k,j}\right|\leq\mu\cdot\max_{j^{\prime}\in[n]}\{(p_{j^{\prime}}\cdot s-d_{j^{\prime}})\},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n].

(68)

where

\mu=\sup_{\bm{v}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{m},\bm{v}^{2},\bm{v}^{3}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\left\{\left\|\begin{aligned} &\bm{v}^{1}\\
&\bm{v}^{2}\\
&\bm{v}^{3}\end{aligned}\right\|_{1}\left|\begin{aligned} &\|(\bm{v}^{1})^{\top}A+(\bm{v}^{2})^{\top}-(\bm{v}^{3})^{\top}\|_{1}=1,\\
&\text{Rows of }\begin{bmatrix}&A\\
&I_{n}\\
&-I_{n}\end{bmatrix}\text{ corresponding to non-zero elements}\\
&\text{of }\begin{pmatrix}&\bm{v}^{1}\\
&\bm{v}^{2}\\
&\bm{v}^{3}\end{pmatrix}\text{ are linear independent.}\end{aligned}\right.\right\}

(69)

A=(a_{i,j})_{\forall i\in[m],j\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and
I_{n}
is an identity matrix with a size
n\times n
.

We do the third step to complete our proof. For any granularity
K
, we let
\{\hat{x}^{K}_{k,j}\}
be a solution of
\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}
(
66
) satisfying

\hat{x}^{K}_{k,j}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p_{j}\cdot s\cdot(q^{K}_{k+1}-q^{K}_{k}),&&\text{if\penalty\ }q^{K}_{k}\geq 1-\hat{q}^{*}_{j}\\
&\left(q^{K}_{k+1}-(1-\hat{q}^{*}_{j})\right)\cdot p_{j}\cdot s,&&\text{if\penalty\ }q^{K}_{k}<1-\hat{q}^{*}_{j}\leq q^{K}_{k+1}\\
&0,&&\text{if\penalty\ }q^{K}_{k+1}<1-\hat{q}^{*}_{j}.\end{aligned}\right.

Since
\{\hat{q}^{*}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
is an optimal solution to
\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}
under the formulation (
65
), we must have
\{\hat{x}^{K}_{k,j}\}
is an optimal solution of
\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}
under the formulation (
66
). Then, we denote by

\{\bar{x}^{K}_{k,j}\}
one optimal solution of
\bar{V}_{\bm{c}}^{K}(\bm{d})
(
67
), as specified in
1
. We further construct

\bar{q}^{K}_{j}=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bar{x}^{K}_{k,j}}{d_{j}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n].

(70)

We denote by
\hat{\bm{q}}^{*}=(\hat{q}^{*}_{1},\dots,\hat{q}^{*}_{n})
and
\bar{\bm{q}}^{K}=(\bar{q}^{K}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}^{K}_{n})
. From definition, we know that
\hat{\bm{q}}^{*},\bar{\bm{q}}^{K}\in[0,1]^{K}
. Therefore, there exists a point
\bar{\bm{q}}^{\prime}
and a sequence of integers
\{K_{1},\dots,K_{w},\dots\}_{w=1,2,\dots}
such that

\bar{\bm{q}}^{\prime}=\lim_{w\rightarrow\infty}\bar{\bm{q}}^{K_{w}}.

(71)

We show in the following claim that
\bar{\bm{q}}^{\prime}
is an optimal solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
12
).

Claim 2

Let
\bar{\bm{q}}^{\prime}
be constructed in (
71
). Then,
\bar{\bm{q}}^{\prime}
is an optimal solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
12
).

For any integer
w
, from
1
, we know that

\displaystyle\|\hat{\bm{q}}^{*}-\bar{\bm{q}}^{K_{w}}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K_{w}}\hat{x}^{K_{w}}_{k,j}}{p_{j}\cdot s}-\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K_{w}}\bar{x}^{K_{w}}_{k,j}}{d_{j}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K_{w}}\hat{x}^{K_{w}}_{k,j}}{p_{j}\cdot s}-\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K_{w}}\bar{x}^{K_{w}}_{k,j}}{p_{j}\cdot s}\right\|_{\infty}+\left\|\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K_{w}}\bar{x}^{K_{w}}_{k,j}}{p_{j}\cdot s}-\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K_{w}}\bar{x}^{K_{w}}_{k,j}}{d_{j}}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{j\in[n]}\left\{\frac{\mu}{p_{j}}\right\}\cdot\max_{j^{\prime}\in[n]}\{(p_{j^{\prime}}-d_{j^{\prime}}/s)\}+\max_{j\in[n]}\left\{\left|\frac{d_{j}}{p_{j}\cdot s}-1\right|\right\}.

Therefore, from (
71
) and
2
, there exists an optimal solution
\bar{\bm{q}}^{\prime}
of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
12
) such that

\|\hat{\bm{q}}^{*}-\bar{\bm{q}}^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\leq\max_{j\in[n]}\left\{\frac{\mu}{p_{j}}\right\}\cdot\max_{j^{\prime}\in[n]}\{(p_{j^{\prime}}-d_{j^{\prime}}/s)\}+\max_{j\in[n]}\left\{\left|\frac{d_{j}}{p_{j}\cdot s}-1\right|\right\}.

Our proof of (
14
) is thus completed. We now bound the constant
\mu
and thus bound the constant
\kappa_{1}
.
From Theorem 2.2 of
Mangasarian and Shiau 1987
, we know that
\mu
in (
69
) is finite. We now derive an upper bound of it.

From Proposition 2.6 of
Mangasarian and Shiau 1987
, we know that

\mu\leq(m+2n)\cdot\Delta A

(72)

where
\Delta A
denotes the maximum of the absolute values of the determinants of the square submatrices of
\begin{bmatrix}&A\\
&I_{n}\\
&-I_{n}\end{bmatrix}
.

We denote by
A^{\prime}
to be a square submatrix of
\begin{bmatrix}&A\\
&I_{n}\\
&-I_{n}\end{bmatrix}
. Following the Hadamard’s inequality on matrix determinant (
Lemma

D.2
), we have that

|\text{det}(A^{\prime})|=\prod_{i\in[n^{\prime}]}\|\bm{a}^{\prime}_{i}\|_{2}

where
n^{\prime}
denotes the size of matrix
A^{\prime}
and
\bm{a}^{\prime}_{i}
denotes the
i
-th column of matrix
A^{\prime}
. Therefore, it is clear to see that

|\text{det}(A^{\prime})|=\prod_{i\in[n^{\prime}]}\|\bm{a}^{\prime}_{i}\|_{2}\leq\bar{a}^{n^{\prime}}\leq\bar{a}^{n}

for any submatrix
A^{\prime}
, which completes our proof.

\square

Proof of
1
.

We denote the linear programming
\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}
(
66
) by

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}=

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{x}

(73)

s.t.

\displaystyle\bm{l}^{\prime}\leq\mathcal{A}\bm{x}\leq\bm{u}^{\prime}

and denote the linear programming
\bar{V}^{K}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
67
) by

\displaystyle\bar{V}^{K}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})=

\displaystyle\max

\displaystyle\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{x}

(74)

s.t.

\displaystyle\bm{l}^{\prime\prime}\leq\mathcal{A}\bm{x}\leq\bm{u}^{\prime\prime}.

Fix an optimal solution
\hat{\bm{x}}^{K}
of
\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}
. We denote by
J_{1},J_{2}
and
J_{3}
three row index sets of
\mathcal{A}
such that

\mathcal{A}_{J_{1}}\hat{\bm{x}}^{K}=\bm{u}^{\prime}_{J_{1}},\mathcal{A}_{J_{2}}\hat{\bm{x}}^{K}=\bm{l}^{\prime}_{J_{2}},\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\bm{u}^{\prime}_{J_{3}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{3}}\hat{\bm{x}}^{K}<\bm{b}^{\prime}_{J_{3}}.

We further fix an optimal solution
\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}
of
\bar{V}^{K}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
and denote by
J_{1}=J_{1,1}\cup J_{1,2}
,
J_{2}=J_{2,1}\cup J_{2,2}
such that

\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,1}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}=\bm{u}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{1,1}},\penalty\ \mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}<\bm{u}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{1,2}},\penalty\ \mathcal{A}_{J_{2,1}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}=\bm{l}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{2,1}}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}>\bm{l}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{2,2}}.

Then, we denote by a set of linear equalities and linear inequalities

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,1}}{\bm{x}}=\bm{u}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{1,1}}

(75)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}\leq\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}{\bm{x}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}{\bm{x}}\leq\bm{u}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{1,2}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,1}}{\bm{x}}=\bm{l}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{2,1}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}\geq\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}{\bm{x}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}{\bm{x}}\geq\bm{l}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{1,2}}

\displaystyle\bm{l}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{3}}\leq\mathcal{A}_{J_{3}}{\bm{x}}\leq\bm{u}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{3}}.

It is clear that
\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}
satisfies the linear system (
75
). On the other hand,
\hat{\bm{x}}^{K}
satisfies the following linear system:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,1}}{\bm{x}}=\bm{u}^{\prime}_{J_{1,1}}

(76)

\displaystyle\bm{u}^{\prime}_{J_{1,2}}-\bm{u}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{1,2}}+\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}\leq\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}{\bm{x}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}{\bm{x}}\leq\bm{u}^{\prime}_{J_{1,2}}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,1}}{\bm{x}}=\bm{l}^{\prime}_{J_{2,1}}

\displaystyle\bm{l}^{\prime}_{J_{2,2}}-\bm{l}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{2,2}}+\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}\geq\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}{\bm{x}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}{\bm{x}}\geq\bm{l}^{\prime}_{J_{2,2}}

\displaystyle\bm{l}^{\prime}_{J_{3}}\leq\mathcal{A}_{J_{3}}{\bm{x}}\leq\bm{u}^{\prime}_{J_{3}}.

Our remaining analysis can be classified into two steps. For the first step, we show that any variable
\bm{x}
that satisfies the linear system (
75
) turns out to be an optimal solution of
\bar{V}^{K}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
74
). For the second step, we show that for
\hat{\bm{x}}^{K}
that satisfies the linear system (
76
), we can find a variable
\bar{\bm{x}}^{K}
satisfying the linear system (
75
) such that (
68
) holds.

We now prove the first step. Since
\hat{\bm{x}}^{K}
is an optimal solution of
\hat{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}
(
73
), from the KKT optimality condition, we know that there exists dual variables
\bm{\mu}_{J_{1}}\geq 0
,
\bm{v}_{J_{2}}\leq 0
such that

\mathcal{A}_{J_{1}}^{\top}\bm{\mu}_{J_{1}}+\mathcal{A}_{J_{2}}^{\top}\bm{v}_{J_{2}}=\bm{h}.

Then, for any
\bm{x}
that satisfies the linear system (
75
), we have

\displaystyle\bm{h}^{\top}\bm{x}

\displaystyle=\bm{\mu}^{\top}_{J_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{1}}\bm{x}+\bm{v}^{\top}_{J_{2}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{2}}\bm{x}=\bm{\mu}^{\top}_{J_{1,1}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,1}}\bm{x}+\bm{\mu}^{\top}_{J_{1,2}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}\bm{x}+\bm{v}^{\top}_{J_{2,1}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,1}}\bm{x}+\bm{v}^{\top}_{J_{2,2}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}\bm{x}

\displaystyle\geq\bm{\mu}^{\top}_{J_{1,1}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,1}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}+\bm{\mu}^{\top}_{J_{1,2}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}+\bm{v}^{\top}_{J_{2,1}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,1}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}+\bm{v}^{\top}_{J_{2,2}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}=\bm{\mu}^{\top}_{J_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{1}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}+\bm{v}^{\top}_{J_{2}}\mathcal{A}_{J_{2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\bm{h}^{\top}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}=\bar{V}^{K}_{t,\bm{c}}(\bm{d})

where the first inequality follows from both
\bm{x}
,
\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}
satisfies the linear system (
75
) and thus
\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,1}}\bm{x}=\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,1}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}=\bm{u}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{1,1}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,1}}\bm{x}=\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,1}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}=\bm{l}^{\prime\prime}_{J_{2,1}}
,
\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}\leq\mathcal{A}_{J_{1,2}}{\bm{x}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}\bar{\bm{x}}^{K^{\prime}}\geq\mathcal{A}_{J_{2,2}}{\bm{x}}
.

It only remains to show the second step. We prove by exploiting the special structure of the linear systems (
75
) and (
76
). Note that under our setting, we have

\mathcal{A}=\begin{bmatrix}&A&\dots&A\\
&&I_{nK}&\end{bmatrix}

where
I_{nK}
denotes an identity matrix with size
nK\times nK
and
A=(a_{i,j})_{i\in[m],j\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
. Also, the linear system (
75
) can be rewritten as

\bm{l}^{1}\leq\mathcal{A}\bm{x}\leq\bm{u}^{1}

(77)

and the linear system (
76
) can be rewritten as

\bm{l}^{2}\leq\mathcal{A}\bm{x}\leq\bm{u}^{2}.

(78)

Then, for the solution
\hat{\bm{x}}^{K}
of the linear system (
78
), we construct
\hat{\bm{y}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
with

\hat{y}_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\hat{x}^{K}_{k,j},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n].

It is clear that
\hat{\bm{y}}
is a solution to the following linear system

\hat{\bm{l}}^{2}\leq\begin{bmatrix}&A\\
&I_{n}\end{bmatrix}\bm{y}\leq\hat{\bm{u}}^{2}

(79)

where
\hat{\bm{l}}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
satisfying

\hat{l}^{2}_{i}=l^{2}_{i},\penalty\ \forall i\in[m]\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\hat{l}^{2}_{n+j}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}l^{2}_{j+kn},\penalty\ \forall j\in[n]

and
\hat{\bm{u}}^{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
satisfying

\hat{u}^{2}_{i}=u^{2}_{i},\penalty\ \forall i\in[m]\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\hat{u}^{2}_{n+j}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}u^{2}_{j+kn},\penalty\ \forall j\in[n].

In the same way, we denote by
\hat{\bm{l}}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
satisfying

\hat{l}^{1}_{i}=l^{1}_{i},\penalty\ \forall i\in[m]\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\hat{l}^{1}_{n+j}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}l^{1}_{j+kn},\penalty\ \forall j\in[n]

and
\hat{\bm{u}}^{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+m}
satisfying

\hat{u}^{1}_{i}=u^{1}_{i},\penalty\ \forall i\in[m]\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\hat{u}^{1}_{n+j}=\sum_{k=1}^{K}u^{1}_{j+kn},\penalty\ \forall j\in[n].

We consider the linear system

\hat{\bm{l}}^{1}\leq\begin{bmatrix}&A\\
&I_{n}\end{bmatrix}\bm{y}\leq\hat{\bm{u}}^{1}.

(80)

From
Lemma

D.1
, we know that for the solution
\hat{\bm{y}}
that satisfies linear system (
79
), there exists a solution
\bar{\bm{y}}
that satisfies linear system (
80
) and there also exists a constant
\mu
such that

\|\hat{\bm{y}}-\bar{\bm{y}}\|_{\infty}\leq\mu\cdot\|(\hat{\bm{l}}^{1},\hat{\bm{u}}^{1})-(\hat{\bm{l}}^{2},\hat{\bm{u}}^{2})\|_{\infty}\leq\mu\cdot\max_{j^{\prime}\in[n]}\{(p_{j^{\prime}}\cdot s-d_{j^{\prime}})\}.

(81)

Note that here the constant
\mu
depends solely on
A
and
I_{n}
and is independent of the granularity
K
. We now construct a solution to the linear system (
77
) from
\bar{\bm{y}}
to complete our proof. For each
j\in[n]
and each
k\in[K]
, we define

\bar{x}^{K}_{k,j}=l^{1}_{j+kn}+(\bar{y}_{j}-\hat{l}^{1}_{n+j})\cdot\frac{u^{1}_{j+kn}-l^{1}_{j+kn}}{\hat{u}^{1}_{n+j}-\hat{l}^{1}_{n+j}}.

It is clear to see that
\bar{\bm{x}}^{K}
satisfies linear system (
77
) and satisfies

\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bar{x}^{K}_{k,j}=\bar{y}_{j},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n].

(82)

Our proof of the second step is completed from (
81
), (
71
) and the fact that
\bar{\bm{x}}^{K}
satisfies linear system (
77
). From the conclusion of the first step, we know that
\bar{\bm{x}}^{K}
is an optimal solution to
\bar{V}^{K}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
and our proof of
1
is thus completed.

\square

Proof of
2
.

For any integer
w
, it is clear to see that
\bar{\bm{q}}^{K_{w}}
is a feasible solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
12
), which implies that the limiting point
\bar{\bm{q}}^{\prime}
is a feasible solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
12
) since the feasible set is closed. We now prove optimality.

It is direct to see
from the construction of the piece-wise linear function
\hat{G}^{K}_{j}(\cdot)
that

\bar{V}^{K_{w}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{w}}\beta^{K_{w}}_{j,k}\cdot\bar{x}^{K_{w}}_{k,j}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot\int^{1}_{q=1-\bar{q}^{K_{w}}_{j}}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq\leq\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})

where the first inequality follows from the definition
\bar{q}^{K_{w}}_{j}=\frac{\sum_{k=1}^{K_{w}}\bar{x}^{K_{w}}_{k,j}}{p_{j}\cdot s}
.

We now show that
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})\leq\bar{V}^{K_{w}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})+\frac{(\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j})\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{u_{j}\}}{K_{w}}
. For an optimal solution
\{\bar{q}^{*}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
of
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
. We construct for any
k\in[K_{w}]
and
j\in[n]

x^{K_{w}}_{k,j}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &p_{j}\cdot s\cdot(q^{K_{w}}_{k+1}-q^{K_{w}}_{k}),&&\text{if\penalty\ }q^{K_{w}}_{k}\geq 1-\bar{q}^{*}_{j}\\
&\left(q^{K_{w}}_{k+1}-(1-\bar{q}^{*}_{j})\right)\cdot p_{j}\cdot s,&&\text{if\penalty\ }q^{K_{w}}_{k}<1-\bar{q}^{*}_{j}\leq q^{K_{w}}_{k+1}\\
&0,&&\text{if\penalty\ }q^{K_{w}}_{k+1}<1-\bar{q}^{*}_{j}.\end{aligned}\right.

It is clear to see that
\{x^{K_{w}}_{k,j}\}
is a feasible solution to
\bar{V}^{K_{w}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
67
) and it holds that

\displaystyle\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot G_{j}(\bar{q}^{*}_{j})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot\hat{G}^{K_{w}}_{j}(\bar{q}^{*}_{j})+\frac{(\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j})\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{u_{j}\}}{K_{w}-n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1}^{K_{w}}d_{j}\cdot\beta^{K_{w}}_{j,k}\cdot x^{K_{w}}_{k,j}+\frac{(\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j})\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{u_{j}\}}{K_{w}-n}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{V}^{K_{w}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})+\frac{(\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j})\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{u_{j}\}}{K_{w}-n}

where the first inequality follows from (
64
). Therefore, we conclude that

\bar{V}^{K_{w}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}p_{j}\cdot s\cdot\int^{1}_{q=1-\bar{q}^{K_{w}}_{j}}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq\leq\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})\leq\bar{V}^{K_{w}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})+\frac{(\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j})\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{u_{j}\}}{K_{w}-n}.

Note that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot\int^{1}_{q=1-\bar{q}^{\prime}_{j}}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq=\lim_{w\rightarrow\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot\int^{1}_{q=1-\bar{q}^{K_{w}}_{j}}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq.

We have

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot\int^{1}_{q=1-\bar{q}^{\prime}_{j}}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq-\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})\right|

\displaystyle=\lim_{w\rightarrow\infty}\left|\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}\cdot\int^{1}_{q=1-\bar{q}^{K_{w}}_{j}}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq-\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{w\rightarrow\infty}\frac{(\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j})\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{u_{j}\}}{K_{w}-n}=0

which implies that
\bar{\bm{q}}^{\prime}
is an optimal solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\bm{d})
(
12
). Our proof is therefore completed.

\square

Proof of
Lemma

3.6
.

Under Case (i) when
\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\geq 1-2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}
,
we know that

\bm{c}\geq p_{j_{t}}\cdot s\cdot\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\cdot\bm{a}_{j_{t}}\geq\frac{p_{j_{t}}\cdot s\cdot\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}{2}\geq\bm{a}_{j_{t}}

when
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}\geq 0
. Therefore, we always have enough remaining capacity to serve query
t
with type
j_{t}
.

Since we have
q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}=1
, we only need to construct a feasible solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
by noting that the term
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
contributes negatively to
\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})
as shown in (
19
).
From the feasibility of
\{\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}\}
, we know that

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot a_{j,i}\cdot\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}+a_{j_{t},i}\cdot\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq c_{i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall i\in[m].

(83)

Note that conditioning on the event
\mathcal{G}
, following (
18
), we have

|\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}|\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(s-1)}{s-1}}

which implies
\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\geq 1-3\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\geq\frac{1}{2}
when
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}
satisfying
\frac{s_{0}}{\log s_{0}}\geq 36\kappa_{1}^{2}
.
We construct the following solution
\{\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j,t}\}
for
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
satisfying

\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j,t}=\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t},\forall j\neq j_{t}\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ and\penalty\ \penalty\ }\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j_{t},t}=\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}+\frac{\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-1}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}.

(84)

Since
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot a_{j,i}\cdot\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j,t}\leq c_{i}-a_{j_{t},i}
for each
i\in[m]
, we know that
\{\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j,t}\}
is a feasible solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
, where
\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j_{t},t}\geq 0
follows from
\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}\geq 1
conditioning on the event
\mathcal{G}
.
Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\right.

(85)

\displaystyle\left.-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G}).

We make the following claim.

Claim 3

For any
q_{1},q_{2}\in[0,1]
, it holds that

\int_{q=q_{1}}^{q_{2}}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq\leq F_{j}^{-1}(q_{1})\cdot(q_{2}-q_{1})+\frac{(q_{2}-q_{1})^{2}}{\alpha}

for any
j\in[n]
, where
\alpha
is the lower bound of the density function
f(\cdot|\bm{a}_{j})
specified in
Section

3
.

The proof of
3
is relegated to
Appendix

E
. Therefore, applying
3
, we have

\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\leq F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})\cdot\frac{1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}+\frac{(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha\cdot\tilde{d}^{2}_{j_{t},t+1}}

(86)

and

\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\leq F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})\cdot(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-1)+\frac{(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}

(87)

by noting that
q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}=1
.
Plugging (
86
) and (
87
) into (
85
), we have

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\frac{(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}+\frac{(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha\cdot\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G})

(88)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\frac{(1-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}+\frac{(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha\cdot\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\log s}{\alpha\cdot s}+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}]+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}}\mid\mathcal{G}\right]

where the second inequality follows from
(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}\leq 2\left((1-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}+(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}\right)
, and
(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}\leq 1
, and the third inequality follows from the condition that
\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\geq 1-2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}
. Therefore, our proof is completed.

\square

Proof of
3
.

Denote by functions

G_{j}(q):=\int_{q^{\prime}=q}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q^{\prime})dq^{\prime},\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall j\in[n].

It is clear that

G_{j}^{\prime}(q)=-F^{-1}_{j}(q)\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }G_{j}^{\prime\prime}(q)=-\frac{1}{f(q|\bm{a}_{j})}\in[-\frac{1}{\alpha},0].

Therefore, from the concavity of
G_{j}(\cdot)
, we have

\int_{q=q_{1}}^{q_{2}}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq=G_{j}(q_{1})-G_{j}(q_{2})\leq F^{-1}_{j}(q_{1})\cdot(q_{2}-q_{1})+\frac{(q_{2}-q_{1})^{2}}{\alpha}

which completes our proof.

\square

Proof of
Lemma

3.7
.

In this case,
since we have
{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}=0
, we only need to construct feasible solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
. Note that conditioning on the event
\mathcal{G}
, following (
18
), we have

|\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}|\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(s-1)}{s-1}}

which implies
\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq 3\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
as long as
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}
satisfying
\frac{s_{0}}{\log s_{0}}\geq 36\kappa_{1}^{2}
.
From the feasibility of
\{\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}\}
demonstrated in (
83
), we construct the following solution
\{\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j,t}\}
for
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
satisfying

\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j,t}=\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t},\forall j\neq j_{t}\text{\penalty\ \penalty\ and\penalty\ \penalty\ }\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j_{t},t}=\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\cdot\frac{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}+1}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}.

(89)

Since
\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot a_{j,i}\cdot\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j,t}\leq c_{i}
for each
i\in[m]
, we know that
\{\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j,t}\}
is a feasible solution to
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
, where
\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j_{t},t}\leq 1
follows from
\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}\geq 1
conditioning on the event
\mathcal{G}
.
Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})

(90)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G}).

Applying
3
, we have that

\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\leq-F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})\cdot\frac{\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}+\frac{(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha\cdot\tilde{d}^{2}_{j_{t},t+1}}

(91)

and

\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\leq F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})\cdot\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}+\frac{(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}.

(92)

Plugging (
91
) and (
92
) into (
90
), we get

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\frac{(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}+\frac{(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha\cdot\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G})

(93)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\frac{(\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}+\frac{(\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}+\frac{1}{\alpha\cdot\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\log s}{\alpha\cdot s}+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}[(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}]+\frac{2}{\alpha}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}\mid\mathcal{G}\right]

where the second inequality follows from
(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}\leq 2\left((\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}+(\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}\right)
and
(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}\leq 1
, and the third inequality follows from the condition that
\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq 2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}
. Our proof is thus completed.

\square

Proof of
Lemma

3.8
.

In Case (iii) when
2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\leq\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq 1-2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}
, we know that

\bm{c}\geq p_{j_{t}}\cdot s\cdot\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\cdot\bm{a}_{j_{t}}\geq p_{j_{t}}\cdot 2\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{s\log s}\cdot\bm{a}_{j_{t}}\geq\bm{a}_{j_{t}}

when
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}
such that
s_{0}\log s_{0}\geq\frac{1}{4\kappa_{1}^{2}}\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{\frac{1}{p_{j}^{2}}\}
. Therefore, we always have enough remaining capacity to serve query
t
with type
j_{t}
.

Note that conditioning on the event
\mathcal{G}
, following (
18
), we have

|\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-\hat{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}|\leq\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log(s-1)}{s-1}}

which implies
\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\leq\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\leq 1-\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}
.

We construct feasible solution
\{\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j,t}\}
for
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}-\bm{a}_{j_{t}}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
following the definition in (
84
). Then,
\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j_{t},t}\geq 0
follows from the fact that

\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}\geq\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}\geq\frac{2}{p_{j_{t}}\cdot(s-1)}\geq\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}

conditioning on the event
\mathcal{G}
, as long as
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}
satisfying
s_{0}\log s_{0}\geq\frac{4}{\kappa_{1}^{2}}\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{\frac{1}{p_{j}^{2}}\}
and
\frac{s_{0}}{\log s_{0}}\geq\max_{j\in[n]}\{\frac{4}{p_{j}^{2}}\}
.

We construct a feasible solution
\{\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j,t}\}
for
\bar{V}^{\mathrm{Semi}}_{\bm{c}}(\tilde{\bm{d}}_{t+1})
following the definition in (
89
). Then,
\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j_{t},t}\leq 1
follows from

\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j_{t},t}\leq\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}+\frac{1}{\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}\leq 1-\kappa_{1}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{\log s}{s}}+\frac{3}{2p_{j_{t}}(s-1)}\leq 1

conditioning on the event
\mathcal{G}
, as long as
s\geq s_{0}
for a constant
s_{0}
such that
s_{0}\log s_{0}\geq\frac{9}{4\kappa_{1}^{2}}\cdot\max_{j\in[n]}\{\frac{1}{p_{j}^{2}}\}
.

Therefore, we have that

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\right.

(94)

\displaystyle\left.-q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j,t}}^{1}nF^{-1}_{j}(q)dq-(1-q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t})\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n}\tilde{d}_{j,t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j,t}}^{1}F^{-1}_{j}(q)dq\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}\cdot\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-\tilde{q}^{\prime}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq+(1-q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t})\cdot\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}\cdot\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-\tilde{q}^{\prime\prime}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G}).

Applying
3
, we get (
86
), (
91
), and

\int_{q=1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}}^{1-{q}^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}}F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(q)dq=F^{-1}_{j_{t}}(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})\cdot(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t})+\frac{(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}.

(95)

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\text{Myopic}_{t}(\pi,\bm{c},\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{j_{t},I_{t+1}}\left[q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t}\cdot\frac{(1-\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha\cdot\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}+(1-q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t})\cdot\frac{(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha\cdot\tilde{d}_{j_{t},t+1}}+\frac{(\tilde{q}^{*}_{j_{t},t}-q^{\pi}_{j_{t},t})^{2}}{\alpha}\mid\mathcal{G}\right]\cdot P(\mathcal{G})

(96)
